1. Suppose all the assumptions of the First Welfare Theorem are true.  
True, False, and Explain:   Markets are Pareto and  Kaldor -Hicks efficient.  
TRUE.  The First Wel fare Theorem assumes no externalities, perfect information, and an 
exogenous price vector, and proves markets are Pareto efficient.  Given these assumptions, 
moreover, people sell ANYTHING they possess that ANYONE else values more highly  than they 
do, so m arkets maximize total surplus and are therefore K -H efficient . 
Most students  answered FALSE, citing the notes on the fact that Pareto efficiency does not imply 
K-H efficiency.  This is true in the real world, but the assumptions of the First Welfare Theor em 
are so unrealistic that both Pareto and K -H efficiency follow ! 


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose all the assumptions of the First Welfare Theorem are true. True, False, and Explain: Markets are Pareto and Kaldor-Hicks efficient.",
    "answer": "TRUE",
    "explanation": "The First Welfare Theorem assumes no externalities, perfect information, and an exogenous price vector, proving that markets are Pareto efficient. Given these assumptions, people sell anything they possess that anyone else values more highly than they do, resulting in markets maximizing total surplus and being Kaldor-Hicks efficient. Although Pareto efficiency does not imply K-H efficiency in the real world, the assumptions of the First Welfare Theorem are so unrealistic that both Pareto and K-H efficiency follow."
  }
]

2. Suppose 4 5% of all agents in an economy have U=ln x + ln y,  and the other 
55% have U=.75  ln x + .25 ln y.   All agents start with one unit of x and two  units 
of y.    
True, False, and Explain:   In general equilibrium, exactly 5 5% of the agents 
consume more y than x.  
FALSE.  100% of agents consume more y than x.  To see this, first calculate the equilibrium price 
vector:  
This implies all agents have income of 1*3.52+2*1=5.52.  Type 1 agents spend 50% of this 
income on x, 50% on y, so they consume 5.52*.5/3.52=.784 x and 5.52*.5/1= 2.76 y, so y>x for 
them.  Type 2 agents spend 75% of their income on x, 25% on y, so they consume 5.52*.75/ 3.52=1.18 x and 5.52*.25/1=1.38 y, so y>x for them too.   62

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose 45% of all agents in an economy have U=ln x + ln y, and the other 55% have U=.75 ln x + .25 ln y. All agents start with one unit of x and two units of y. True, False, and Explain: In general equilibrium, exactly 55% of the agents consume more y than x.",
    "answer": "FALSE",
    "explanation": "In fact, 100% of agents consume more y than x. To understand this, we must first calculate the equilibrium price vector, which implies all agents have an income of 1*3.52 + 2*1 = 5.52. Type 1 agents spend 50% of their income on x and 50% on y, resulting in consumption of 5.52 * 0.5 / 3.52 = 0.784 x and 5.52 * 0.5 / 1 = 2.76 y. Thus, y > x for type 1 agents. Similarly, type 2 agents spend 75% of their income on x and 25% on y, resulting in consumption of 5.52 * 0.75 / 3.52 = 1.18 x and 5.52 * 0.25 / 1 = 1.38 y. Therefore, y > x for type 2 agents as well."

3. Suppose that two players play the following three games in order : 
Coordination game, Prisoners' Dilemma , Ultimatum game . 
True, False, and Explain:  Standard game theory predicts that this game will  
completely  "unravel."  
TRUE.  “Unraveling” means players play each stage of the game as if it were not repeated.  The 
last stage – the Ultimatum game – has only one SGPNE, where Player 1 offers $.01 and keeps 
the rest for himself, and Player 2 accepts.  A s a result, standard game theory says players cannot 
use punishments to avoid the standard (Defect, Defect) outcome in the PD stage.  This in turn 
implies that players treat the first stage – the Coordination game – as if it were a one -shot game.   
Hence, c omplete unraveling.  


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose that two players play the following three games in order: Coordination game, Prisoners' Dilemma, Ultimatum game. True, False, and Explain: Standard game theory predicts that this game will completely unravel.",
    "answer": "TRUE",
    "explanation": "Unraveling means players play each stage of the game as if it were not repeated. The last stage, the Ultimatum game, has only one Subgame Perfect Nash Equilibrium (SGPNE), where Player 1 offers $0.01 and keeps the rest for himself, and Player 2 accepts. As a result, standard game theory says players cannot use punishments to avoid the standard (Defect, Defect) outcome in the Prisoners' Dilemma stage. This in turn implies that players treat the first stage, the Coordination game, as if it were a one-shot game. Hence, complete unraveling."
  }
]

6.  Consider a Cournot model with two firms.  P=20 -Q, and MC =0.  
True, False, and Explain:   If one firm moves first, the unique SGPNE has a 
higher Q than it does with simultaneous play.  
TRUE.  With simultaneous play, the Cournot notes tell us market  output is 2/3*20=13.3.  With 
sequential play, player 2 sets q2=(20 -q1)/2, so player 1 maximizes Pq1=( 20-q1-(20 -q1)/2)q1.  
Differentiating implies 10 -q1=0, so q1=10, and q2=(20 -10)/2=5.  So q1+q2=15<13.3.   63 


---

  {
    "enunciate": "Consider a Cournot model with two firms. P=20 -Q, and MC =0. True, False, and Explain: If one firm moves first, the unique SGPNE has a higher Q than it does with simultaneous play.",
    "answer": "TRUE",
    "explanation": "With simultaneous play, the Cournot notes tell us market output is (2/3)*20=13.3. With sequential play, player 2 sets q2=(20 -q1)/2, so player 1 maximizes Pq1=(20-q1-(20 -q1)/2)q1. Differentiating implies 10 -q1=0, so q1=10, and q2=(20 -10)/2=5. So q1+q2=15<13.3."

1. Construct a “second -best” efficiency defense of ONE existing policy you 
consider highly inefficient  in a “first -best” sense .  Be as convincing as possible.  
Under current U.S. law, extremely rare, highly unpopular religions like Satanism are legal.  This 
seems highly inefficient in a first -best sense: the total willingness of religious people to pay to ban 
Satanism probably vastly exceeds the totally willingne ss of Satanists to pay to keep their religion 
legal.  However, politically drawing the line between religions like Satanism and moderately 
unpopular religions like Scientology is extremely contentious, and risks banning religions that DO 
pass the cost -bene fit test because their followers love them and they only mildly annoy the 
majority.  These costs and risks make across -the-board religious toleration a second -best 
optimum: While we have to tolerate a few inefficient religions, we avoid religious conflict and “false 
positives.”  


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Construct a second-best efficiency defense of ONE existing policy you consider highly inefficient in a first-best sense. Be as convincing as possible.",
    "answer": "Under current U.S. law, extremely rare, highly unpopular religions like Satanism are legal.",
    "explanation": "Though allowing unpopular religions like Satanism may seem inefficient in a first-best sense, politically drawing the line between them and moderately unpopular religions is contentious. It risks banning religions that pass the cost-benefit test because their followers value them despite being mildly annoying to the majority. To avoid religious conflict and false positives, across-the-board religious toleration is considered a second-best optimum, even if it means tolerating some inefficient religions."

2. “[I]t seems that the basic textbook commentary on bilateral monopoly and bargaining had it 
right.” (Kreps, A Course in Microeconomic Theory )  Carefully explain (a) what Kreps is 
claiming here, and (b) why he claims it.  Does a MS NE view of bargaining lead to 
a different conclusion?  
Kreps is claiming that, according to standard textbook treatments, “bargaining outcomes depend 
on individual’s expectations as to what the outcomes should be”; furthermore, such expectations 
can be “ma nipulated.”  He claims this because experiments show precisely this: Playing 
bargaining games with computers that demand 20% of the surplus subsequently leads human 
beings to bargain harder with other humans than playing bargaining games with computers tha t 
demand 50% of the surplus.  The MSNE view leads to a somewhat different conclusion: While 
expectations do matter, some expectations are not stable .  In particular, if people expect too 
much, lowering your expectations leads to higher payoffs – and if peo ple expect too little, raising 
expectations leads to higher payoffs.  


---

[{"enunciate": "Carefully explain (a) what Kreps is claiming here, and (b) why he claims it.", "answer": "(a) Kreps is claiming that standard textbook treatments suggest bargaining outcomes depend on individuals' expectations of the outcomes, which can be manipulated. (b) He claims this because experiments show that playing bargaining games with computers demanding different percentages of the surplus influences how hard human beings bargain with other humans.", "explanation": "Kreps argues that, according to basic textbook discussions on bilateral monopoly and bargaining, the outcomes of such negotiations depend on the participants' expectations, and these expectations can be influenced. He supports this claim with experimental evidence that shows when humans play bargaining games with computers demanding different shares of surplus, their subsequent bargaining behavior with other humans is affected."}, {"enunciate": "Does a MS NE view of bargaining lead to a different conclusion?", "answer": "Yes, the MSNE view leads to a somewhat different conclusion.", "explanation": "The Mixed Strategy Nash Equilibrium (MSNE) view of bargaining suggests that while expectations do matter in determining outcomes, not all expectations are stable. If people expect too much, reducing their expectations can lead to higher payoffs, and if they expect too little, raising expectations can result in better outcomes."}]

3. What is the most  empirically relevant model our class has studied so far?  The 
least  empirically relevant model?  Justify both answers.  
The reputation model is the most empirically r elevant.  Reputation makes the business world go 
round, especially in the internet age of ubiquitous product reviews and ratings.  Businesses can 
almost always raise their SHORT -RUN profits by cutting corners today, but refrain from doing so 
because this d estroys their LONG -RUN profits.  
The ultimatum game model is the least empirically relevant.  It clearly predicts something that 
essentially NEVER occurs: offering people in disadvantageous bargaining positions an amount 
vanishingly close to zero.  Instead , human beings in such situations gravitate toward even splits.  


---

[{"enunciate": "What is the most empirically relevant model our class has studied so far? The least empirically relevant model? Justify both answers.", "answer": "The reputation model is the most empirically relevant, and the ultimatum game model is the least empirically relevant.", "explanation": "The reputation model is considered the most relevant because reputation plays a significant role in business, especially in the internet age where product reviews and ratings are easily accessible. Businesses often avoid cutting corners to protect their long-term profits. On the other hand, the ultimatum game model is considered the least relevant because it predicts a behavior that rarely occurs in real-life situations, i.e., offering people in disadvantageous bargaining positions an amount close to zero. In reality, people tend to gravitate towards even splits in such situations."}]

1. Suppose a million new immigrants enter the U.S., driving down the wages of 
low -skilled Americans by $1/hour.  
True, False, and Explain:  According to Landsburg ( Fair Play ), this change 
usually increases Kaldor -Hicks effic ien cy, because the positive 
externalities of  population growth hugely  outweigh the negati ve externality 
of falling wages for natives.  
FALSE.  Landsburg says that falling wages are not  a negative externality at all; instead, they’re a 
TRANSFER to capitalis ts.  As long as there is any demand elasticity, moreover, total surplus 
expands at the lower wage, so Kaldor -Hicks efficiency automatically increases.  Landsburg 
discuses the positive externalities of popula tion growth elsewhere, but they’re  not relevant  here.

    "answer": "FALSE",
    "explanation": "Landsburg argues that falling wages are not a negative externality; instead, they are a transfer to capitalists. 2.  Two types of agents consume guns (g) and butter (b).  The Type A agents 
have U=.7 ln g + .3 ln b, and initially own 1 unit of guns and 1 unit of butter.  The 
Type B agents have U=.3 ln g + .7 ln b, and initially own .5 units of guns and .5 
units of butter.  50% of the agents are Type A; the other 50% are Type B.  
True, False, and Explain:  In GE, t he price of guns will not equal the price of 
butter.  
TRUE.  In GE, PriceG/PriceB=(.5*.7*1+.5*.3*.5)/(.5*.3*1+.5*.7*.5)=.425/.325=1.31>0.  


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: In General Equilibrium (GE), the price of guns will not equal the price of butter.",
    "answer": "TRUE",
    "explanation": "In General Equilibrium, the ratio of the prices of guns to butter can be computed using the utility functions of Type A and Type B agents, and their initial endowments. The price ratio of guns to butter (PriceG/PriceB) is given by the following equation: ((0.5 * 0.7 * 1) + (0.5 * 0.3 * 0.5)) / ((0.5 * 0.3 * 1) + (0.5 * 0.7 * 0.5)) = 0.425 / 0.325 = 1.31 > 0. Since the price ratio of guns to butter is greater than 0, the price of guns will not equal the price of butter in General Equilibrium."
  }

4. Suppose two players play the following normal form N times.  N is finite and 
known by both players.  
 Left  Right  
Left  5,0  0,0  
Right  0,0  0,5  
True, False, and Explain:  Alternating back and f orth between (L,L) and (R,R) 
is always an equilibrium as long as N is even . 
FALSE.  Alternating back and forth is always an equilibrium for ALL N.  There is no incentive to 
cheat in the last turn, since defection automatically yields a payoff of 0.  This is particularly clear 
because (L,L) and (R,R) are both equilibria off the stage game, and repeatedly playing stage 
game equilibria is always an equilibrium.  


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose two players play the following normal form N times. N is finite and known by both players. True, False, and Explain: Alternating back and forth between (L,L) and (R,R) is always an equilibrium as long as N is even.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Alternating back and forth between (L,L) and (R,R) is always an equilibrium for all values of N, not just even N. There is no incentive for the players to cheat during the last turn, as defecting would result in a payoff of 0. This can be better understood by acknowledging that (L,L) and (R,R) are both equilibria in the stage game, and playing these stage game equilibria repeatedly will always lead to an equilibrium."
  }
]

6.  True, False, and Explain:   The War/Peace game confirms the view that the 
invention of atomic weapons reduced  the risks of war and  human 
extinction.   
FALSE.  The PSNE of the War/Peace game remains (Peace, War) and (War, Peace) no matter 
how bad the (War, War) payoffs become .  But for the MSNE, the frequency of playing War falls as 
the (War, War) payoffs fall, and the frequency of the (War, War) result falls quadratically.  
However, the risk of actual war only declines because at omic weapons make actual war so 
terrible IF it occurs!  With more primitive weapons, it is unclear if human extinction was even a 
possible outcome of war.  Now, it may be.  


---

[{"enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: The War/Peace game confirms the view that the invention of atomic weapons reduced the risks of war and human extinction.", "answer": "FALSE", "explanation": "The Pure Strategy Nash Equilibrium (PSNE) of the War/Peace game remains (Peace, War) and (War, Peace) regardless of how bad the (War, War) payoffs become. However, in the Mixed Strategy Nash Equilibrium (MSNE), the frequency of playing War decreases as the (War, War) payoffs decrease, and the frequency of the (War, War) result decreases quadratically. The risk of actual war declines primarily because atomic weapons make war disastrous if it occurs. With more primitive weapons, it's unclear if human extinction was even a possible outcome of war, but now, it may be possible."}]

1.  People (including economists) often think that monopolies are productively 
inefficient.  Why, on reflection, would this be strange?  What additional 
assumptions do you need to make the “inefficient monopolies” stor y internally 
consistent?  
Productively ineffici ent monopoly is strange because – for ALL market structures – higher costs 
imply lower profits.  Even if a firm’s monopoly is guaranteed by the government, why not maximize 
profits by minimizing costs?  The tw o simplest ways to make this story internally consistent: (1) 
The monopolist could have lobbied to legally exclude more productively efficient competitors from 
the industry.  (2) The monopolist’s profits might be capped by the government, giving the 
monopo list an incentive to stealthily take its profits in the form of higher costs (the “ gold -plating  
effect”).  


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "People (including economists) often think that monopolies are productively inefficient. Why, on reflection, would this be strange? What additional assumptions do you need to make the inefficient monopolies story internally consistent?",
    "answer": "Productively inefficient monopoly is strange because for all market structures, higher costs imply lower profits.",
    "explanation": "It is strange to think that monopolies are productively inefficient because in all market structures, having higher costs would result in lower profits. To make the story of inefficient monopolies internally consistent, one could consider the following assumptions: (1) The monopolist may have lobbied to exclude more efficient competitors from the industry legally. (2) The monopolist's profits might be capped by the government, giving the monopolist an incentive to take its profits in the form of higher costs, known as the 'gold-plating' effect."
  }
]

What’s striking is that sharply  deviating from many  extreme assumptions of supply -and -demand 
doesn’t seem to matter much for mark et outcomes.  So even though the Walrasian model seems 
to poorly describe the real world , the model still correctly predicts market outcomes; subjects act 
“as if” they’re perfect competitors even when they’re not.  This suggests that industries 
economists usually describe as “oligopolistic” may actually be just as “competitive” as atomistic 
industries.  And when I look around at the world, this seems plausible: As a rule, number of firms 
seems like a poor predictor of how well firms treat consumers.

1. All agents in an economy have U=ln x + ln y. 25% have 1 unit of x and 0 units of y. 75% have 0 units of x and 1 unit of y.

True.
a_i=0.5, b_i=0.5
25% of agents have y_1=0, x_1=1
75% of agents have y_2=1, x_2=0
      P_x/P_y =(· a_i ??y_i^ ?_ ?_ )/(· b_i ?x_i^ ?_ )
=(0.25)(0.5)(0)+(0.75)(0.5)(1)Ö(0.25)(0.5)(1)+(0.75)(0.5)(0) 
=0.375Ö0.125
=3

    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "Given that 25% of agents have 1 unit of x and 0 units of y, and 75% have 0 units of x and 1 unit of y, we can calculate P_x/P_y as (a_i * y_i)/(b_i * x_i) = (0.25 * 0.5 * 0) + (0.75 * 0.5 * 1) / (0.25 * 0.5 * 1) + (0.75 * 0.5 * 0), which simplifies to 0.375 / 0.125, resulting in a value of 3. 3. "The economically correct argument is the most politically incorrect argument imaginable." (Landsburg, The Armchair Economist)
True, False, and Explain: Landsburg is explaining that laws restricting women's use of cosmetic surgery make women in general worse off.
False. *Pg. 220-222
Women are not necessarily made worse off by laws restricting women's use of cosmetic surgery--specifically breast implants. Landsburg says that what matters may not be overall breast size, but relative breast size, so a ban on breast implants may not make women worse off in the dating market necessarily. If regulations were such that only one woman could get breast implants, she would be better off in the dating market, and all other women slightly disadvantaged, but not terribly. Instead, a ban on breast implants would make society overall worse off. Because men and the casual observer derive benefits from women getting breast implants, a ban on breast implant would reduce the overall benefits to society. 
P. 221: Cost-benefit analysis: 
- Men get benefits from women getting breast implants
- The casual observer gets benefits from women getting breast implants
- Women[1][2]
4[3]. A group of N oligopolists producing identical products plays a finite number of turns. Two of the players are then randomly selected to play a Hawk-Dove game ONCE.
True, False, and Explain: With rational, selfish players, some degree of collusion may be sustainable under Cournot competition, but NOT Bertrand competition.
True. Cournot case can have tacit collusion even in a one shot game, but in Bertrand case you either have explicit collusion or the perfectly competitive outcome. Working backwards, the outcome of Hawk-Dove isn't going to affect the outcome.

6. In a "market for lemons," the true value of cars to sellers is uniformly distributed on the interval [0,100]. Buyers value cars at 25% more than sellers. Now suppose that buyers in this market do NOT have rational expectations, but sellers do. To be specific, buyers believe the quality of the average car available for sale is as high as the quality of the average car not available for sale.
True, False, and Explain: This market with asymmetric information and irrational expectations is as efficient as a comparable market with symmetric information and rational expectations.
See 2009 final
False. In theory, irrationality could increase or decrease market efficiency; in practice, experiments show that irrationality makes the "market for lemons" less efficient. 
This market with asymmetric information and irrational expectations is not as efficient as market with symmetric information and rational expectations, but it is more efficient than a market with asymmetric info and rational expectations (this market "unravels").
True. We argue that both markets will clear. The first one, where irrationality exists, will clear but with a transfer to sellers. The second market, where there is symmetric imperfect information, will clear fairly easily.[5]

7. After several decades, the government finally eliminates sugar subsidies in 2005. The sugar industry is thrown into a severe crisis and many plantations go bankrupt. 
True, False, and Explain: This is strong evidence that sugar executives did not have rational expectations about government sugar policy.
False. *week 8 RE definition from notes  -  essentially, while you expect people to reasonably estimate the outcomes of certain events, doesn't mean these estimates are perfect. 
RE means that people's subjective probability = the objective probability. But it does not rule out error or suggest that information or cognition is free. So, while someone with RE may be expected to reasonably estimate the outcome of future events, it does not preclude error. The experts may not have known that the subsidies were going to end.

    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Rational expectations means that people's subjective probability equals the objective probability, but it does not rule out error or suggest that information or cognition is free. In this case, while sugar executives might have been expected to reasonably estimate the outcome of future events, it does not mean their estimates were perfect. They may not have known that the subsidies were going to end, leading to the crisis in the sugar industry."

9. In a "psychologist/engineer" problem, the base rate is 70% engineers, 30% lawyers. Fred's P(having a pocket protector| a person is an engineer)=.5, and P(a person is an engineer|having a pocket protector)=.8. If the base rate is changed to 30% engineers, 70% lawyers, Fred's P(a person is an engineer|having a pocket protector) falls to .3.
True, False, and Explain: Fred does NOT suffer from representativeness bias.
True. *Week 11 notes. Fred adjusts for base rates. 
Fred does not suffer from representativeness bias because his P(a person is an engineer|having a pocket protector) adjust for a change in the base rate. 
Bayes' Rule: P(A|B)=(P(B|A)P(A))/(P(B|A)P(A)+P(B|~A)P(~A))
E-30% L-70%
P(A|B)=(0.5(0.3))/((0.5)(0.3)+0.5(0.7))=0.15/0.5=0.3
Fred's P(a person is an engineer|having a pocket protector)=0.3, thus Fred does not suffer from representativeness bias.


---

[
{
"enunciate": "In a \"psychologist/engineer\" problem, the base rate is 70% engineers, 30% lawyers. Fred's P(having a pocket protector| a person is an engineer)=.5, and P(a person is an engineer|having a pocket protector)=.8. If the base rate is changed to 30% engineers, 70% lawyers, Fred's P(a person is an engineer|having a pocket protector) falls to .3. Does Fred suffer from representativeness bias?",
"answer": "False",
"explanation": "Fred does not suffer from representativeness bias because he adjusts his probability for a person being an engineer given they have a pocket protector according to changes in the base rates. By applying Bayes' Rule, we can see that Fred's updated probability equals 0.3, which shows he takes the base rate into account and thus does not suffer from representativeness bias."
}

11. True, False, and Explain: The "liquidity constraints" and "debt aversion" explanations for observed departures from consumption smoothing are empirically indistinguishable.
False. You can vary the amount of collateral and see how much people actually borrow -- econ grad student vs. tenure econ professor. Can more objectively tell if someone is liquidity constrained or not and compare. *Week 13 notes
If liquidity constraints were the only issue, then we would see individuals with higher expected future incomes maxing out their credit (or approaching it), but we don't (or at least not very often). Additionally, we would see more retirees taking advantage of reverse mortgages; "debt aversion" cannot explain their hesitancy to avail themselves of this option since they wouldn't incur any debt by doing so. Thus, there are instances where we can distinguish between liquidity constraints and debt aversion.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: The \"liquidity constraints\" and \"debt aversion\" explanations for observed departures from consumption smoothing are empirically indistinguishable.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "We can observe differences between liquidity constraints and debt aversion by analyzing the borrowing behavior of individuals with varying collateral, such as an econ grad student compared to a tenure econ professor. If liquidity constraints were the only issue, individuals with higher expected future incomes would frequently max out their credit, which is not often observed. Additionally, if it was just about debt aversion, retirees would be more willing to use reverse mortgages as they wouldn't incur any debt. Therefore, there are instances where we can distinguish between liquidity constraints and debt aversion."

2. Suppose your EU=w.3, and insurance is sold at 10% more than the actuarially fair rate. Your uninsured income is $100,000 with p=.5, and $50,000 with
p=.5. Using this information, do the following: (a) Calculate your coefficient of absolute risk aversion(CARA). (b) Solve for your optimal quantity of insurance, i.
(a)CARA=?-U?^"/U^' 
U=w^0.3
U^'=0.3w^(-0.7)
U^"=-0.3*0.7w^(-1.7)
CARA=(0.3*0.7w^(-1.7))/(0.3w^(-0.7) )=0.7w^(-1)
	This indicates that the agent is risk averse.
(b): #'s are too difficult to solve problem without calculator
	?Max?_i  0.5?(100,000-0.5*1.1i)?^0.3+0.5?(50,000+1-0.5*1.1i)?^0.3[8]
-.55*.3*.5(100,000-.55i)-0.7 + .45*.3*.5(50,000+.45i)-0.7
.0825(100,000-.55i)-.7 = .0675(50,000+.45i)-.7
(11/9)(100,000-.55i)-.7 = (50,000+.45i)-.7
.751((100,000-.55i)= 50,000+.45i
75,100-.41305i=50,000+.45i
25,100 = .86305i
i = 29,082.90

    "explanation": "To calculate the coefficient of absolute risk aversion (CARA), we first find the first and second derivatives of the utility function U = w^0.3. The first derivative is U' = 0.3w^(-0.7), and the second derivative is U'' = -0.3 * 0.7w^(-1.7). CARA is defined as the ratio of the second derivative to the first derivative, so CARA = (-0.3 * 0.7w^(-1.7)) / (0.3w^(-0.7)) = 0.7w^(-1). This expression indicates that the agent is indeed risk averse."
  },
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose your EU = w^0.3, and insurance is sold at 10% more than the actuarially fair rate. Your uninsured income is $100,000 with p = 0.5, and $50,000 with p = 0.5. (b) Solve for your optimal quantity of insurance, i.",
    "answer": "i = 29,082.90",
    "explanation": "To solve for the optimal quantity of insurance (i), we need to maximize the expected utility function: 0.5(100,000 - 0.55 * 1.1i)^0.3 + 0.5(50,000 + 1 - 0.5 * 1.1i)^0.3. Taking the derivative with respect to i and setting it to zero, we get a simplified equation: 0.0825(100,000 - 0.55i)^(-0.7) = 0.0675(50,000 + 0.45i)^(-0.7). Solving for i, we obtain i = 29,082.90. This is the optimal quantity of insurance for the agent."
  }

4. Caplan argues that systematically biased beliefs about economics lead to inefficient policies. Part of the motivation for his survey of intelligence research is his view that public opinion has systematically biased views on this topic as well. In a democracy, what consequences of these biases might Caplan predict for education policy? How would Wittman respond? Who is right?
Caplan may argue that the public may systematically have negatively biased views about intelligence research. This may have consequences for education policy - people may support more funding for education than is necessary, given that may overestimate the benefits that come from education, when some of the benefits are actually driven by intelligence regardless of education. This may well result in deadweight loss - if the signaling model is true or even partially true, it would be efficient for the state to tax education instead. Wittman would argue that, while this may be true in the short run, in the long run it would tend towards efficiency. In the short run we may inefficiently subsidize education, but this will come out in the wash.[10] I believe the evidence supports Caplan's point. Voters do hold systematically biased beliefs, and political systems give them no good reasons to update them, thus leaving us with potentially inefficient policies.

1. According to Caplan (SEJ 1999), "[I]f there is a small probability that one is delusional, the perception of a zero-probability event would rationally provoke one to doubt one's own perception rather than believe that the impossible has occurred."
True, False, and Explain: Caplan's conclusion can be logically deduced from Bayes' Rule if delusional people have a strictly positive probability of thinking they observe 0-probability events, non-delusional people have a 0 probability of thinking they observe 0-probability events, and P(you're sane)>P(you're insane)>0.
Refer to the impossible event as D. Your prior probability is P(D)=0. After thinking you saw the impossible event, your conditional probability that the impossible has occurred is P(D|B)=(P(B|D)P(D))/P(B)=0. So you do not Bayesian update in favor of the impossible having occurred. 
By contrast, consider the situation where B is perceiving the impossible event, and A  is being insane. In this case, P(B|~A)=0,  so P(A|B)=(P(B|A)P(A))/((P(B|A)P(A))=1, so you will Bayesian update to become CERTAIN that you are insane! QED
True. *Pg. 831 footnote #17  -  "If one literally assigned a probability of zero (or failed to assign a positive probability) to an event that actually happens, one would be yet more surprised. But, continuity aside, why would a rational agent assign a zero probability to a conceivable event? Moreover, if there is a small probability that one is delusional, the perception of a zero-probability event would rationally provoke one to doubt one's own perception rather than believe that the impossible has occurred."


---

So you do not Bayesian update in favor of the impossible having occurred. Conversely, consider the situation where perceiving the impossible event is B and being insane is A. In this case, P(B|~A)=0, so P(A|B)=1, and you will Bayesian update to become certain that you are insane."}]

2. Suppose that two players play this PD game 100 times in a row:
Player 2
Player 1
Coop
Defect
Coop
2,2
0,3
Defect
3,0
1,1
Afterwards, they simultaneously play an Ultimatum game where Player 1 splits a total payoff of 1 between himself and Player 2. 
True, False, and Explain: Cooperation in this game will "unravel" due to backwards induction.
False.  True. PD game 100x in a row would unravel. Now, if they simultaneously play ultimatum game at the end cooperation will still unravel. The subgame equilibrium of the Ultimatum game will not change the outcome of PD game, because there is no way to punish a defector. The Ultimatum game only has one NE, so there's no way to enforce cooperation in the preceding PD game. 
We agree with your reasoning, but this should be True, right? It will unravel. 
See similar question #3 Midterm 2016:
 Suppose that two players play the following three games in order: Coordination game, Prisoners' Dilemma, Ultimatum game.
True, False, and Explain: Standard game theory predicts that this game will completely "unravel."
TRUE.  "Unraveling" means players play each stage of the game as if it were not repeated.  The last stage  -  the Ultimatum game  -  has only one SGPNE, where Player 1 offers $.01 and keeps the rest for himself, and Player 2 accepts.  As a result, standard game theory says players cannot use punishments to avoid the standard (Defect, Defect) outcome in the PD stage.  This in turn implies that players treat the first stage  -  the Coordination game  -  as if it were a one-shot game.  Hence, complete unraveling.

    "explanation": "Cooperation in this game will unravel due to backwards induction. The PD game being played 100 times in a row would typically unravel on its own. There is an industry with a fixed cost and constant MC. 2 firms simultaneously decide whether to enter the industry. Firms set prices, not quantities.

3. True, False, and Explain: If the fixed costs are NOT sunk, there are two PSNE. If the fixed costs ARE sunk, there is a MSNE but no PSNE.
False. Simultaneous decision to incur sunk costs has 2 PSNE. *Week 5 notes, section 5i -- note that fixed costs which are NOT sunk = they're avoidable. Fixed costs that  are sunk = they're not avoidable. 


4.True, False, and Explain: The outcome of the game is more efficient if costs are sunk.
 True. That's the only game where you get enter, enter and price falls to MC.[11][12] The outcome enter, enter doesn't occur when costs are NOT sunk. 
Under monopoly, total surplus is (Pm+ CSmon) with probability 1. -  So when is contestability welfare-dominated by simple monopoly? -  When the expected surplus under contestability is less than (Pm + CSmon):
 - (-2a + CSmax) + 2 (Pm+ CSmon) <  - (Pm+ CSmon)
Thus, suppose that Pm=a. -  Then contestability is welfare-dominated if:
.25* (-2a + CSmax) + .25* (a + CSmon) < (a + CSmon)
which reduces to:
a > CSmax  - - -  3*CSmon
i.e., monopoly welfare dominates if the fixed costs exceeds the consumer surplus under competition minus three times the consumer surplus under monopoly.

6. Suppose that everyone overestimates their personal probability of death by 50%.
True, False, and Explain: There will be propitious selection[13] in the market for life insurance.
False? Because the question says EVERYONE overestimates. 		
*Longevity expectations may play a role in determining health insurance choices, though the net effect of higher longevity expectations on investment in health is theoretically ambiguous. Those who expect to live longer may want to spend more now on their health since such investment will pay dividends over a longer horizon. Thus, they would demand more insurance. However, the marginal value of additional current health investment may be lower when a long life already seems likely.  
"The theory of propitious selection suggests that there are risk-avoiding personalities who both take physical precautions and buy financial security (insurance). Conversely, there are risk seekers who tend to do neither. Survey evidence is presented that is consistent with the theory. Individuals who obtain motor vehicle liability coverage are less likely than others to drink-and-drive, and are more likely to engage in health-beneficial (risk-avoiding) behaviors. Propitious selection may be a general phenomenon promoting favorable selection in many real world insurance markets." https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00057881 


---

Longevity expectations may influence health insurance choices, but the net effect of higher longevity expectations on investment in health is theoretically ambiguous. Propitious selection suggests that there are risk-avoiding personalities who take physical precautions and buy financial security (insurance) and risk seekers who tend not to. However, in this scenario, the overestimation of personal probability of death affects everyone, not just specific risk-taking personalities."}]

7. "Most things in life don't turn out as well as you thought they would. While psychologists, poets, and philosophers have often remarked on this phenomenon, few have recognized that it is a necessary consequence of informed, rational decision making." (Landsburg, The Armchair Economist)
True, False, and Explain: If by "rational," Landsburg means "rational expectations," his last statement is incorrect.
*Chap. 18, first paragraph on pg. 224 (My pagination has it on page 174)
False. The fact that most things in life don't turn out as well as you thought they would is still consistent with rational expectations. Rational expectations does not mean that you will be correct all the time--instead, your judgments are unbiased. Your mean error will be zero, but there is variance about this mean due to differing levels of information held by individuals. This "problem" is compounded when selecting things that you like--one is likely to select the objects that they think are among the best, which are also the most likely to disappoint (your errors will tend to be overestimates rather than underestimates.) 


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: If by \"rational,\" Landsburg means \"rational expectations,\" his last statement is incorrect.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "The fact that most things in life don't turn out as well as you thought they would is consistent with rational expectations. Rational expectations does not mean that you will be correct all the time, but rather that your judgments are unbiased. Your mean error will be zero, but there is variance around this mean due to differing levels of information held by individuals. This issue is compounded when selecting things that you like, as one is likely to select the objects that they believe are among the best, which are also the most likely to disappoint (your errors will tend to be overestimates rather than underestimates)."
  }

8.True, False, and Explain: Caplan (Stigler-Becker vs. Myers-Briggs) claims that empirical personality research strongly contradicts the standard economic assumption of stable preferences.
False. Preferences are, empirically, quite stable. However, they are far from identical and have proven predictive power for economically interesting variables. The empirical challenge for future research is to jointly estimate the impact of preferences and constraints to obtain unbiased measures of their relative importance. 
Caplan does not dispute the stability of preferences. He critiques the homogeneity of preferences between agents. S&B assert that preferences are homogeneous, therefore consumer choice is explained solely by issues that are to be found in the budget constraints. However, evidence suggests, strongly, that agents with the same endowments may make different choices because of preference differences (heterogeneity) even though they same agents do not change their choices over time (stability). 
"On a more applied level, the overall findings in personality psychology suggest that Stigler and Becker's view of preferences is half-right and half-wrong. Empirically, there is fairly strong support for the view that preferences are stable; personality changes are rare, especially after the age of 30 (McCrae and Costa, 1990). However, the view that preferences are identical is very difficult to empirically defend. Rather, personalities differ widely along a handful of basic dimensions (Piedmont, 1998; Costa and McCrae, 1995; Johnson, 1997). In fact, much "pathological" behavior that non-economists cite as evidence against the economic approach is easy to reinterpret as standard behavior by actors from extreme tails of the personality distribution (Costa and Widiger, 1994; Costa and McCrae, 1992)." (pg. 2 of article, pg. 392 of journal)[14]


---

Stigler and Becker argue that preferences are homogeneous and consumer choice is determined solely by budget constraints. However, evidence shows that agents with the same endowments may make different choices due to preference heterogeneity, even if their choices don't change over time. 9. A critic of behavioral economics runs the following experiment. Half of the subjects guess the populations of twenty countries. The remaining subjects try to list as many people as they can from each of the twenty countries. The true populations of these countries are known to the experimenter.
True, False, and Explain: This data can be used to test for availability bias.
False? A better test for availability bias would have the subjects guess the populations of 
countries they are most knowledgeable about. The participant should have a high correlation between their knowledge of the country and the value of the estimated population.[15]
This test asks about number of people who can be named, but a key thing for the availability heuristic is ease and fluency of recall. If people can name lots of people but find it very difficult, availability bias might make them less likely to see the population as large, rather than more. Number of names and ease of naming must be correlated for this to be an effective test.
Scott: I think that another important aspect is that you need to have the group who were asked to name as many people from a country as they could be the ones who estimate the population. 11. True, False, and Explain: A standard neoclassical agent with diminishing marginal utility of wealth and access to perfect capital markets will always have a constant level of consumption.
A standard neoclassical agent will smooth consumption and conform to the permanent income hypothesis. One objection that might be raised is that sometimes an individual will receive windfall or unexpected gains, altering their permanent income. But assuming the individual has rational expectations, the deviations in income from their expectations will not be systematically biased upward or downward[16].  
False. Under those assumptions, we can expect the agent to have a constant level of consumption. However, we cannot expect the agent to ALWAYS have a constant level of consumption. The agent will have a constant level of consumption plus or minus a random term. The agent will update expectations for permanent income in each period. In the limit, we can expect these changes in expectation to be equally distributed around zero, but we cannot expect this in each period.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: A standard neoclassical agent with diminishing marginal utility of wealth and access to perfect capital markets will always have a constant level of consumption.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Although a standard neoclassical agent with diminishing marginal utility of wealth will smooth consumption and conform to the permanent income hypothesis, we cannot expect the agent to always maintain a constant level of consumption. The agent's consumption will be constant plus or minus a random term, as they update their expectations for permanent income in each period. In the long run, these changes in expectations will be equally distributed around zero, but we cannot expect a constant level of consumption in every period."
  }
]

12. True, False, and Explain: Caplan ("Rational Irrationality and the Microfoundations of Political Failure") argues that asymmetric information leads rational voters to vote for policies that encourage rent-seeking.
False. Caplan cites Breton & Winsrobe (1982) who argues that when asymmetric info gets worse, public demand for action goes down, not up. 
Think of a market for lemons; the problem with asymmetric information is that it deters exchange, not that it incentivizes it. 
Caplan grants Wittman's argument that given competition among  political actors (such as competitive elections), rational expectations by voters, and political institutions that reduce transaction costs will lead to political "markets" being efficient. Where he differs from Wittman is that instead of concluding democracy is efficient, Caplan argues that voters do not have rational expectations. 
Analytical questions:

2.) Suppose you are a fully rational individual living in a closed society like North Korea where all news is tightly monitored by the government. Explain in detail how you would go about forming rational beliefs under these conditions. What are the main conclusions you would draw?
As Wittman argues (pg. 1401), "biased sources of information need not lead to biases in belief." Individuals will discount information from sources that they know to be biased. This applies even in a totalitarian society. As Wittman puts it in a footnote, also on pg. 1401:
"Even when the ruling class has a virtual domestic monopoly on the instruments of information, as is the case in the Soviet Union, we observe people discounting the information contained in their papers and trusting foreign sources (e.g., when the Chernobyl nuclear accident occurred, citizens of the Soviet Union turned to foreign sources for their information)." 
In other words, to form rational beliefs in North Korea, I would heavily discount information and arguments presented in the media, doing very little to update my beliefs on the basis of the information North Korean media outlets produced. If possible and not too risky/personally costly, I would seek foreign sources of news. If not, I would take positions that I held on the basis of my expectation prior to seeing media accounts. Assuming I have rational expectations, my errors will be randomly distributed. State media, on the other hand, displays systematic bias, and therefore would tend to have its errors distributed in one direction. In a system with North Korean state media as my only information source, a rational individual would have low epistemic confidence in one's opinions and would expect to have high variance in one's opinion's distance from the truth. 
The main conclusions I would draw would be that: My mean opinion would still be correct, but my variance would be higher than that of an individual with access to reliable sources of information, and therefore any given opinion I hold would be more likely to be further from the truth. I would also conclude that I should not update my priors based on information I receive from the North Korean media, as updating based on that information would introduce systematic error. I would therefore retain the same opinions I started with, but would do so with low confidence. 


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose you are a fully rational individual living in a closed society like North Korea where all news is tightly monitored by the government. Explain in detail how you would go about forming rational beliefs under these conditions. What are the main conclusions you would draw?",
    "answer": "To form rational beliefs in North Korea, I would heavily discount information and arguments presented in the media, doing very little to update my beliefs on the basis of the information North Korean media outlets produced. If possible and not too risky/personally costly, I would seek foreign sources of news. If not, I would take positions that I held on the basis of my expectation prior to seeing media accounts. My mean opinion would still be correct, but my variance would be higher than that of an individual with access to reliable sources of information, and therefore any given opinion I hold would be more likely to be further from the truth.",
    "explanation": "As Wittman argues, biased sources of information do not necessarily lead to biased beliefs. Individuals can discount information from known biased sources, even in a totalitarian society. In the case of North Korea, a rational individual would heavily discount information from state media and seek foreign sources if possible. By doing this, they can form beliefs based on their own expectations and maintain a certain level of rationality. However, their opinions would likely have higher variance due to the limited access to reliable information, leading to lower confidence in their beliefs."
  }

3. -  What would prevent Beckerian optimal punishment from driving efficiency wages down to the market-clearing level? -  Discuss at least TWO mechanisms.
Key takeaways: liquidity constraints, and imperfect enforcement. Thank you based Kroencke.
- High enforcement costs and legal inability to create the corresponding high punishments
- Bonds would be one way out in the existing legal regime which prevents punitive contracts, however the particulars would likely get bogged down in court. 
http://www.nber.org/papers/w2356.pdf
These are my notes from meeting with Caplan: "Certainty and severity are the mechanisms. We want PD to get really high. To keep them from operating as they should there needs to be caps on these: either preventing punishments from getting harsher or the certainty of catching people."

[{"enunciate": "What would prevent Beckerian optimal punishment from driving efficiency wages down to the market-clearing level? Discuss at least TWO mechanisms.", "answer": "Liquidity constraints and Imperfect enforcement", "explanation": "Liquidity constraints may limit the ability of firms to implement optimal punishment, while imperfect enforcement might arise from high enforcement costs and legal inability to create the corresponding high punishments. Additionally, bonds could potentially address these issues in the existing legal regime that prevents punitive contracts, but their implementation could get bogged down in court. Another perspective is that caps on certainty and severity of punishments could prevent them from being as effective as they should be, either by limiting the harshness of punishments or the probability of catching wrongdoers."}]

4. How does the endowment effect influence democratic politics? Discuss in general terms, and give the most plausible example you can. How might a paternalistic economist take advantage of the electorate's endowment effect? A self-interested demagogue?
Because of the endowment effect, people place more value on objects simply because they view those objects as theirs. Public goods, government services, or entitlement programs that individuals view as belonging to them will be more difficult to get rid of. A self-interested demagogue  
- Some regulation/ regulatory agencies could be seen as an individual's mechanism for ensuring, food safety for able
- Public Parks
- City/ state provided libraries
      Paternalistic economist: We can imagine a paternalistic economist believes that people should read more, and as such, he supports a law promoting the building of new libraries. The economist wants to make sure his policy "sticks", so he would want to make sure that this new library is viewed as part of a person's endowment. Once it is viewed as part of their endowment, it is less likely the library will be defunded or taken away, thus increasing the chances of the economist's policy being successful. 
      "If we take away people's library, they won't have access to learning resources. Because people need to read more 
      (1) more people should read, (2) if there's no library, kids will be getting into trouble rather than reading, (3) poor people need access to the internet and computer that they wouldn't have otherwise
      Self-interested demagogue: Goal: get elected/ re-elected
      "They're going to take away your library, I'll make sure that doesn't happen."
      If we take away the library, we're taking away children's access to learning programs, community development 


---

    "answer": "The endowment effect influences democratic politics by making people place more value on objects or services they view as theirs, like public goods, government services, or entitlement programs. A paternalistic economist might take advantage of the endowment effect by promoting a policy, such as building libraries, and ensuring it becomes part of people's endowment. A self-interested demagogue might use the endowment effect to gain support by promising to protect these valued services, like libraries.",
    "explanation": "The endowment effect causes people to value objects or services they view as part of their endowment. In the context of democratic politics, this implies that public goods, government services, or entitlement programs that individuals see as their own are harder to remove. A paternalistic economist could exploit this by promoting a policy like building libraries and ensuring they become part of people's endowment, making it less likely for the libraries to be defunded. A self-interested demagogue could gain political support by promising to protect these valued services from being taken away, such as libraries and the resources they provide."

1. Suppose you have a betting market for the 2004 presidential election. The price of "Bush wins" shares sell for twice as much as "Bush does not win" shares.
True, False, and Explain: As long as Arrow-Debreu assumptions and expected utility theory hold, this shows that the marginal trader believes that Bush will win with p=2/3.


---

  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose you have a betting market for the 2004 presidential election. The price of \"Bush wins\" shares sell for twice as much as \"Bush does not win\" shares. True or False: As long as Arrow-Debreu assumptions and expected utility theory hold, this shows that the marginal trader believes that Bush will win with p=2/3.",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "According to Arrow-Debreu assumptions and expected utility theory, agents maximize their expected utility in a perfectly competitive market. When the price of \"Bush wins\" shares is twice the price of \"Bush does not win\" shares, it indicates that the marginal trader believes that the probability of Bush winning is 2/3, since the expected payoff of both shares would be equal in order for the market to clear."

2. Suppose that two players play the following three games in order: Ultimatum game, Coordination game, Prisoners' Dilemma.
True, False, and Explain: Standard game theory predicts that this game will completely "unravel."
True. "Unraveling" means players play each stage of the game as if it were not repeated. In the last stage, the PD game, the outcome will be defect, defect no matter what. 


---

[{"enunciate": "Suppose that two players play the following three games in order: Ultimatum game, Coordination game, Prisoners' Dilemma. Does standard game theory predict that this game will completely 'unravel'?", "answer": "True", "explanation": "According to standard game theory, 'unraveling' implies that players play each stage of the game as if it were not repeated. In the last stage, which is the Prisoners' Dilemma game, the outcome will always be defect, defect regardless of prior actions."}]

3. Suppose you have a 2-player version of the voluntary donation game from the notes, with one difference: Each agent cares somewhat about the other, so player one maximizes U1=c1D+.7*c2D, and player two maximizes U2=c2D+.7*c1D. 
True, False, and Explain: In symmetric Nash equilibrium, c1=c2>4/7.
U1=c1D+.7c2D
=(c1+.7c2)D
=(c1+.7c2)(1-c1+1-c2)
=(c1+.7c2)(2-c1-c2)
?U1/?c1=(2-c1-c2)-(c1+.7c2)=0
2c1=2-.3c2
c1=1-.15c2=1-.15(1-.15c1) because c2 is symmetric with c1
c1=1-.15-.0225c1
1.0225c1=.85
c1 = .831, which is greater than 4/7[17]


---

[
  {
    "enuncate": "Suppose you have a 2-player version of the voluntary donation game from the notes, with one difference: Each agent cares somewhat about the other, so player one maximizes U1=c1D+.7*c2D, and player two maximizes U2=c2D+.7*c1D. In symmetric Nash equilibrium, is c1=c2>4/7?",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "Player one maximizes U1=(c1 + 0.7c2)D and player two maximizes U2=(c2 + 0.7c1)D. In symmetric Nash equilibrium, c1 and c2 should be equal. When finding the Nash equilibrium, we derive U1 with respect to c1 and set it to 0. U1 = (c1 + 0.7c2)(2 - c1 - c2). The derivative of U1 with respect to c1 is (2 - c1 - c2) - (c1 + 0.7c2) = 0. Solving for c1, we get c1 = 1 - 0.15c2, and since c1 and c2 are symmetric, c1=1 - 0.15(1 - 0.15c1). Solving for c1, we get c1 = 0.831, which is greater than 4/7."
  }
]

Step 2: If you selected the second option, you are allowed to buy an "unemployment insurance" policy. It costs $10,000 and pays $25,000 if you are unemployed.
Step 3: You find out if you got a job. If you did not, you collect the insurance payout. Either way, you have to pay the premium.
True, False, and Explain: You will take the sure thing.
 False. E(U) of sure thing: ($50k).6=659.75
E(U) of second option with insurance: .6(100k-10k).6+.4(25k-10k).4=691.39
E(U) of second option without insurance: .6(100k).6=600

9. Rationally ignorant voters could punish dishonest politicians by increasing the punishment for hard-to-detect offenses.
True, False, and Explain: One reason that fully rational voters might fail to use this strategy is a coordination problem.
True. See page 325 of "Rational Irrantionality and MicrofoundationsÉ". "Why then would a majority of rational voters oppose economic reforms with negative costs and positive benefits? Resolving this puzzle without abandoning voter rationality is challenging but not impossible. Theorists raise several possibilities. Voters might simply be risk-averse. Voters and reformers could be playing a complicated game of asymmetric information and signaling (e.g., Alesina and Drazen, 1991). Different voting blocs might suffer from coordination problems (e.g., Lab‡n and Sturzenegger, 1994). Fernandez and Rodrik (1991) show that even with risk-neutral voters, uncertainty about the identity of the gainers and losers could be a sufficient explanation." (emphasis added)
2005 FINAL EXAM

***

There are two kinds of workers, good and bad.  Both types are equally numerous.  Good workers are worth $100k to me; bad workers are worth $25k to me.  It costs good workers $25k to complete school, but $50k for bad workers to do so.  I can tell if a worker finished school, but cannot observe their quality directly.  Workers earn 75% of their value to me if they choose to be self-employed.


5. True, False, and Explain: I will not hire any workers, so asymmetric information entails no deadweight cost.
False. Although I may not hire any good workers (since they can make $75,000 on their own and would have to be paid at least $100k to work for me if I can only identify them by their having a degree), it's still worth it to hire bad workers. They can make $18,750 on their own, but they are worth $25k to me, so I can make approximately $6,250  for each bad worker. If there were not asymmetric information problems and I could identify who the good and bad workers are, then I would hire good workers and they would generate $100k of wealth instead of $75k on their own. Thus the asymmetric information entails a deadweight cost.


6. Suppose I can find out a worker's true type by paying $15k.
True, False, and Explain: I will never pay this fee, because my expected gross profit per worker is only 50%*($100k-$75k)=12.5k.
False. If I can make ~$25k per good worker and pay $15k to figure out whether she is good, I net $10k. If I find out he's a bad worker, I lose $8,750 ($6,250-$15k). Thus, the expected gross profit from paying the fee is .5($1,250)=$625.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: I will not hire any workers, so asymmetric information entails no deadweight cost.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Although you may not hire any good workers (since they can make $75,000 on their own and would need to be paid at least $100k to work for you if identified only by having a degree), it's still worth it to hire bad workers. Bad workers can make $18,750 on their own, but they are worth $25k to you, so you can make approximately $6,250 for each bad worker. If there weren't any asymmetric information problems and you could identify the good and bad workers, then you would hire good workers and they would generate $100k of wealth instead of $75k on their own. Hence, the asymmetric information causes a deadweight cost."
  },
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose I can find out a worker's true type by paying $15k. True, False, and Explain: I will never pay this fee, because my expected gross profit per worker is only 50%*($100k-$75k)=12.5k.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "If you can make about $25k per good worker and pay $15k to determine whether they are good, you net $10k. If you find out they are a bad worker, you lose $8,750 ($6,250-$15k). Therefore, the expected gross profit from paying the fee is 0.5($1,250)=$625."

There are two kinds of workers, good and bad.  Both types are equally numerous.  Good workers are worth $100k to me; bad workers are worth $25k to me.  It costs both kinds of workers $5k to finish school. I can tell if a worker finished school, but cannot observe their quality directly.  Workers earn 40% of their value to me if they choose to be self-employed. Finally, assume that if a worker fails to go to school, I automatically conclude that he is bad.


5. True, False, and Explain: Everyone goes to school and earns the same wage.
 False? *Week 9 notes, IV A
False. We can offer bad workers $40k to induce them to not get a degree, and pay those with a degree $45k to only attract good workers. In this situation, our profit per good worker is $55k and loss per bad worker is $15k. If we were to offer degree holders $45k and pay anything less than $40 to non-degree holders, then everyone would get a degree and we would lose $20k per bad worker. Therefore, it is in our interest to offer non-degree holders $40k to reduce our losses from bad workers.



6. Suppose workers earn 80% of their value to me if they are self-employed.
True, False, and Explain: Only bad workers will go to school, and will earn a net wage (wages minus schooling costs) of $20k.
False? *Week 9 notes We would be best off just offering a wage of $20k to all workers. If we try to create a differential wage for educated workers then we will lose more money on the bad workers than we gain from the good workers.


---

[{"enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Everyone goes to school and earns the same wage.", "answer": "False", "explanation": "We can offer bad workers $40k to induce them not to get a degree, and pay those with a degree $45k to only attract good workers. In this situation, our profit per good worker is $55k and loss per bad worker is $15k. If we were to offer degree holders $45k and pay anything less than $40k to non-degree holders, then everyone would get a degree and we would lose $20k per bad worker. Therefore, it is in our interest to offer non-degree holders $40k to reduce our losses from bad workers."}, {"enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Only bad workers will go to school, and will earn a net wage (wages minus schooling costs) of $20k.", "answer": "False", "explanation": "We would be best off just offering a wage of $20k to all workers. If we try to create a differential wage for educated workers, then we will lose more money on the bad workers than we gain from the good workers."}]

8. Suppose that education causes a temporary increase in intelligence, but the effect "fades-out" after a year. 
True, False, and Explain:  Controlling for intelligence, the estimated effect of education is downwardly biased.
Scott would answer that this is true. Education drives some of the increase in IQ. When estimating how much you are now earning, some of this will be attributed to IQ and some will be attributed to education. But, if the increase is only because you were educated, this is underestimating the effect education has on earnings, because some of the effect is being picked up by IQ. Scott may be wrong.
 *pg. 645 intelligence paper. Rate of return to education estimates that fail to control for intelligence are likely to be biased upwards.

9. Caplan argues that irrationality has psychological benefits for many if not most people.
True, False, and Explain: Economic theory cannot say whether the private psychological benefits of irrationality exceed its social costs.
Nathan would say: True. However,  while economic theory cannot offer us a definitive answer, economic theory coupled with empirical evidence of the institutional specifics can give us good hints. Under institutions where the social costs of irrationality are largely internalized, such as the market, we would expect the psychological benefits of irrationality that persists to exceed the social costs. However, in democratic systems people do not internalize the costs of their irrationality. Political outcomes are not directly changed by an individual's vote, meaning there is zero cost to a voter indulging in irrationality. Yet the total social costs can be tremendous. Since the social costs are not internalized, we have good reasons to believe that the private psychological benefits of irrationality are exceeded by their social costs. Nathan might be wrong.[20]
2007 FINAL EXAM


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Caplan argues that irrationality has psychological benefits for many if not most people. True, False, and Explain: Economic theory cannot say whether the private psychological benefits of irrationality exceed its social costs.",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "Economic theory itself cannot provide a definitive answer about whether the private psychological benefits of irrationality exceed its social costs. However, when combined with empirical evidence and considering the specifics of institutions, we can gain useful insights. In market systems, where the social costs of irrationality are largely internalized, the psychological benefits of persistent irrationality could outweigh the social costs. In contrast, in democratic systems, individuals do not typically bear the consequences of their irrationality, as their individual votes do not directly change political outcomes. This means there is little to no cost for a voter to indulge in irrationality, but the aggregate social costs can be significant. Given that these social costs are not internalized, it's reasonable to assert that the private psychological benefits of irrationality may be surpassed by their social costs. Nevertheless, there might be cases where this statement is incorrect."
  }
]

5. Consider a risk-neutral farmer with the cost function TC=q.5.  The market price is 3 with p=.5, and 0 with p=.5.  
True, False, and Explain: If the farmer has RE, he sets q=1/3. If the farmer's beliefs do not satisfy RE (he thinks the price=3 with p=.2), his profits fall by 50%.
I think this question is wrong (and that this is not trick). 
MC= .5/q^.5, E(Px)=1.5
1.5=.5/q^.5
(1.5)q^.5=.5
.5/1.5 =q^.5
? =q^.5
Therefore q* with RE should be (?)^2 
I would find q* without RE using E(Px)= .6, i.e. (.2)(3)
.6=.5/q^.5
.6(q^.5)=.5
q^.5=.5/.6
q*=(.5/.6)^2
After that I would look at profit in both situations. 
*HW #6 -- similar question

    "enunciate": "Consider a risk-neutral farmer with the cost function TC=q^0.5. The market price is 3 with p=0.5, and 0 with p=0.5. True, False, and Explain: If the farmer has RE, he sets q=1/3.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "The given cost function is TC=q^0.5. In a risk-neutral situation with rational expectations (RE), the farmer sets his quantity produced based on the expected price for the product. The expected price (E(Px)) equals 1.5, since the price is 3 with p=0.5 and 0 with p=0.5. The marginal cost (MC) is 0.5/q^0.5. To find the optimal quantity (q*) with RE, we set E(Px) equal to MC. Therefore, q*.5 = 0.5/1.5 or q* = (0.5/1.5)^2, which is not equal to 1/3."

6. True, False, and Explain: Landsburg argues that cosmetic surgery is socially wasteful signaling.
The answer to this is in Chapter 17 (Courtship and Collusion) in Landsburg's Armchair Economist (pages 221-222.) 
False
Landsburg specifically discusses breast implants. If the value of a breast size increase is not absolute breast size but relative breast size, then women will find themselves in an arms race in which they engage in socially wasteful spending in order to better attract partners. In this case, taxing or prohibiting the breast implants could lead to efficiency improvements, just like taxing education could if education's private returns come in the form of signaling rather than improving human capital.  However, Landsburg notes that it is likely not entirely this, because there are benefits to onlookers, possible benefits to women unrelated to attracting men by increasing their relative position, and so on[21].  Also, what Landsburg is discussing is an instance of a socially wasteful positional good, but not necessarily signaling. Signaling involves doing something costly that indicates what type you are in an instance of asymmetric information. Breast implants are not indicating what type you are, but instead are positioning you as having larger breasts than your competitors in the mating market, thereby appealing to the preferences of men who prefer women with "the largest breasts in the room" (Landsburg's phrase).


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Landsburg argues that cosmetic surgery is socially wasteful signaling.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Landsburg focuses on the case of breast implants, discussing the idea that if the value of breast size increase relies on relative size rather than absolute size, women might engage in a kind of arms race involving socially wasteful spending to attract partners. However, he also notes that there are other potential benefits, such as enjoyment for onlookers or personal satisfaction unrelated to attracting partners. Moreover, while breast implants might represent a socially wasteful positional good, they don't necessarily serve as signaling, since they don't convey information about the individual's type in a situation with asymmetric information."
  }
]

False: Thaler believes that there are some cases where NOT acting like a standard economic agent is beneficial--for example, when deciding to cooperate in certain situations. Economic assumptions are "normative" in that they describe how homo economicus should act, but not prescriptive in telling how people actually act.

8. Consider an efficiency wage model.
True, False, and Explain:  As the average worker becomes less selfish, the unemployment rate falls. As the average worker becomes more myopic, the unemployment rate rises. 
True. The efficiency wage model states that workers may be paid a higher wage to increase the threat of firing and lead to less shirking. A less selfish employee will not need to be "threatened" to work as hard as possible for the company--lower wages can be paid, thus lowering the unemployment rate. A myopic employee will need to be paid this efficiency wage, leading to higher unemployment as jobs become more scarce relative to those seeking them and increasing the threat of a firing.
I interpret "myopic" to mean that people that currently hold jobs don't think about the time they may be unemployed in the future and how their behavior today (getting paid efficiency wages) may affect their employment prospects down the line.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Consider an efficiency wage model. True, False, and Explain: As the average worker becomes less selfish, the unemployment rate falls. As the average worker becomes more myopic, the unemployment rate rises.",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "In an efficiency wage model, workers are paid higher wages as an incentive to increase their productivity and reduce shirking. When workers are less selfish, they do not require such incentives to work hard, and therefore, employers can pay lower wages. This results in a reduction in the unemployment rate. On the other hand, when workers are more myopic, they fail to consider the long-term consequences of their actions, which makes it necessary for employers to pay efficiency wages to encourage productivity. This leads to higher unemployment, as jobs become more scarce relative to those seeking them, and it also increases the threat of being fired."
  }
]

9. True, False, and Explain: If the efficient markets hypothesis is correct, then the marginal private cost of irrational investing can easily be zero.
True. Under the efficient markets hypothesis, asset prices changes should be completely unpredictable--asset prices should follow a random walk. If this is true, then there is no payoff to rational investing--if you indulge your own irrational preferences, you are likely to do as well as anyone else. A more viable strategy would be to purchase index funds and hedge against variance. 
 2008 FINAL EXAM


---

[{"enunciate": "If the efficient markets hypothesis is correct, then the marginal private cost of irrational investing can easily be zero.", "answer": "True", "explanation": "According to the efficient markets hypothesis, asset price changes are unpredictable and follow a random walk. Consequently, there is no significant advantage to rational investing. Indulging in irrational preferences may yield similar results. A more practical approach would be to invest in index funds and hedge against variance."}]

2. True, False, and Explain: In The Armchair Economist, Landsburg defends the market as a Darwinian mechanism that weeds out inefficiency.
False. Landsburg says that the analogy of markets being like Darwinian evolution fails because the Darwinian mechanism doesn't ensure efficient outcomes - male birds of paradise have ridiculously long tails (lemme smash) that hinder them and it would benefit all of them to agree to not have long tails, so Darwinian evolution leads to an inefficient outcome. It is necessary to have prices so that competition can lead to efficient outcomes.[22]


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: In The Armchair Economist, Landsburg defends the market as a Darwinian mechanism that weeds out inefficiency.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Landsburg argues that the analogy of markets being like Darwinian evolution is flawed because the Darwinian mechanism doesn't guarantee efficient outcomes. For example, male birds of paradise have excessively long tails that hinder them, and it would be beneficial for all of them to agree not to have long tails. Therefore, according to Landsburg, Darwinian evolution can lead to inefficient outcomes, and prices are needed for competition to result in efficiency."
  }

4. Suppose a woman has EU=w.5 + X. If the woman has a baby boy, X= -10; if she has a baby girl, X=5; if she has no child, X=0. Normally, she is equally likely to have a boy or a girl. But if she pays a price P, she can determine her childs gender with certainty. Suppose further that w initially equals $400, and the P is the only cost associated with having children.
True, False, and Explain: The woman will remain childless if P>87.5.
False. The price of abortion indifference is $175. She will choose to have a child at any price <$175. [23]
Without abortion:
EU=(400)^.5+.5(-10)_+ .5(5)=17.5
Childless:
EU=(400)^.5=20
With abortion:
EU=(400-p)^.5+5
If p=174, then EU=20. Therefore, at any p<175 you will choose to have a child.

    "enunciate": "Suppose a woman has EU=w.5 + X. If the woman has a baby boy, X= -10; if she has a baby girl, X=5; if she has no child, X=0. Normally, she is equally likely to have a boy or a girl. True, False, and Explain: The woman will remain childless if P>87.5.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "We can determine the woman's expected utility (EU) in three different scenarios: without abortion, childless, and with abortion. Without abortion, the EU is calculated as (400)^0.5 + 0.5(-10) + 0.5(5) = 17.5. While being childless, the EU is (400)^0.5 = 20. With abortion, the EU is (400-p)^0.5 + 5. 9. Suppose Caplan is right that voters buy large quantities of irrational political beliefs.
True, False, and Explain: This hardly shows that political markets are inefficient. Instead, it shows that we have mismeasured their primary output; the main product of political markets is psychological well-being rather than wealth-maximizing policy.
False? Saying that markets are or are not efficient is a very specific claim. By appealing to Kaldor-Hicks efficiency, we are arguing that they maximize the total social value of resources. Markets that do not do this may be many things, but we cannot say they are efficient. This is Scott's answer--and Scott thinks it is bad.   Nathan adds: We could note that voter's psychological well-being can factor into efficiency if voters place a dollar-value on that psychological well-being. However, because the fact that your vote will not decide an election means voters can indulge their irrationality up to their bliss point with a price of zero, there is no reason to think that the psychological benefits of the irrationality to them exceed the social costs. This is a classic externality problem, in which the social costs are positive but the private costs to the decision-maker are zero.
2009 FINAL EXAM


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose Caplan is right that voters buy large quantities of irrational political beliefs. True, False, and Explain: This hardly shows that political markets are inefficient. Instead, it shows that we have mismeasured their primary output; the main product of political markets is psychological well-being rather than wealth-maximizing policy.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Efficiency in markets refers to the maximization of total social value of resources. While psychological well-being of voters could factor into efficiency if given a monetary value, the fact that an individual's vote does not decide an election allows voters to indulge in their irrationality without consequence. This leads to an externality problem where the social costs exceed the private costs to the decision-maker, making the claim that these political markets are efficient debatable."
  }
