2. Suppose there are an infinite number of equally efficient potential producers.
True, False, and Explain: With constant MC and zero fixed costs, there are an infinite number of PSNE and an infinite number of MSNE.
  True. Any number of firms greater than one can enter the market and regardless of the number, every firm will remain indifferent to entering or exiting, i.e. unilaterally changing their decision, because at P=MC, profits will be zero.

[{"enunciate": "Suppose there are an infinite number of equally efficient potential producers. 5. True, False, and Explain: Moral hazard models predict that the people with the most insurance will take the most risks, but adverse selection models predict the opposite.
False. Moral hazard models do predict that, ceteris paribus, those with more insurance will take more risks, but adverse selection does not predict the opposite. Rather, it predicts that people who are "less risky" than average will find insurance to be a bad deal and choose to forgo it, leaving only riskier individuals remaining in the pool. Therefore, igiven the market doesn't completely unravel, only risky people will hold insurance.


---

{
"questions": [
{
"enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Moral hazard models predict that the people with the most insurance will take the most risks, but adverse selection models predict the opposite.",
"answer": "False",
"explanation": "Moral hazard models do predict that, all other things being equal, those with more insurance will take more risks. However, adverse selection does not predict the opposite. Adverse selection models predict that people who are less risky than average will find insurance to be a bad deal and choose not to purchase it, leaving only riskier individuals remaining in the insurance pool. As a result, if the market doesn't completely unravel, only risky people will hold insurance."
}
]
}

6. More educated workers normally have lower unemployment rates than less educated workers.
True, False, and Explain: The efficiency wage model can explain this pattern.
 False. 
True, the efficiency wage can explain the pattern. It is not too heroic an assumption to imagine that monitoring costs of white-collar salaried workers are lower than those of monitoring blue collar workers who are paid by the hour.  More educated workers are more likely to be paid based on their observable output rather than less observable input. In this situation there is no reason to pay an efficiency wage, which is paid to overcome high monitoring costs. When efficiency wages are paid by all firms results in effective wage floor leaving some workers unemployed(so that firms can give credible threats to employees caught shirking). In this situation, the presence of efficiency wages in the less educated labor market and the lack of efficiency wages in the more educated labor market explains the pattern of unemployment rates.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "More educated workers normally have lower unemployment rates than less educated workers. Is this statement true or false, and can the efficiency wage model explain this pattern?",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "The efficiency wage can explain the pattern. Monitoring costs of white-collar salaried workers are likely lower than those of blue-collar hourly workers. More educated workers are more likely to be paid based on their observable output rather than less observable input. In this situation, there is no need to pay an efficiency wage, which is paid to overcome high monitoring costs. Efficiency wages, when paid by all firms, result in an effective wage floor leaving some workers unemployed (so that firms can give credible threats to employees caught shirking). In this situation, the presence of efficiency wages in the less educated labor market and the lack of efficiency wages in the more educated labor market explains the pattern of unemployment rates."
  }
]

Despite the public goods themselves, benefitting society not just the selfish agent, the signalling value can be captured by the agent.

5. Consider two professors. John earns $55,000 paid in monthly installments. Joan earns a base salary of $45,000 paid over twelve months, and a guaranteed extra $10,000 paid during the summer months. (Thaler, The Winner's Curse)
True, False, and Explain: Thaler explains that the mental accounting prediction is that Joan will outspend John during the summer, but John will outspend Joan throughout the rest of the year.
False[26]. Thaler holds that the mental accounting model indicates that Joan's spending will always be lower than John's. This conclusion comes from separating the monthly pay and lump-sum pay into two accounts: the income-flow account and the asset account. Empirical estimates show that the income-flow's MPC is higher than the assert accounts MPS. Therefore, since the level Joan consumes will always be lower than jown, so will her other spending, i.i. saves most of lump-sum payments. 


---

[{"enunciate": "Consider two professors. John earns $55,000 paid in monthly installments. Joan earns a base salary of $45,000 paid over twelve months, and a guaranteed extra $10,000 paid during the summer months. True, False, and Explain: Thaler explains that the mental accounting prediction is that Joan will outspend John during the summer, but John will outspend Joan throughout the rest of the year.", "answer": "False", "explanation": "Thaler argues that the mental accounting model indicates Joan's spending will always be lower than John's. This conclusion comes from separating the monthly pay and lump-sum pay into two accounts: the income-flow account and the asset account. Empirical estimates show that the income-flow's marginal propensity to consume (MPC) is higher than the asset account's marginal propensity to save (MPS). Therefore, since Joan's consumption level will always be lower than John's, her other spending will be lower as well, saving most of her lump-sum payments."}]

5. Suppose foregone earnings are the sole cost of education, and people live forever. Without school, workers earn $10,000 per year; every year of school completed boosts earnings by 10%. 
True, False, and Explain: Without externalities, the social rate of return to education is 10%.
False. The question says nothing about the nature of the relationship between school year completion and the increase in earnings. If the value of education is at least partially as a signal, an extremely low bar,  the social rate of return will be lower rate of return. The larger amount of earnings explained by the signal of education rather than human capital gains, the lower the social rate of return. In the real world, an accurate accounting of costs and benefits often leads to estimates of a negative rate of social return for education.

1. True, False, and Explain:  A lump-sum tax is theoretically the most efficient way to raise a given amount of government revenue, but it is impractical in the real world.
This is a Landsburg question, I believe.
True. Taxation is theft. A lump-sum tax (or head tax) incurs no deadweight costs relative to  other methods of taxation because individuals cannot avoid the tax and will therefore not expend resources to do so (page 74, Why Taxes are bad, Armchair economist.)
.
It is impractical in the real world because of widespread equity concerns (it is more obviously regressive). 


---

  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: A lump-sum tax is theoretically the most efficient way to raise a given amount of government revenue, but it is impractical in the real world.",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "A lump-sum tax is considered the most efficient way to raise government revenue because it incurs no deadweight costs compared to other taxation methods. Individuals cannot avoid the tax and will not expend resources trying to do so. However, it is impractical in the real world due to widespread equity concerns, as it is more obviously regressive."
  }
]

4.  True, False, and Explain:  Efficiency wages cease to encourage work effort if all employers pay efficiency wages. 
False. Employees will still have the incentive to work hard, as finding a new job if you are fired becomes more difficult. The higher wage across the entire industry means that finding a job will be more difficult, as your marginal productivity must now be higher to secure a job when the wage is above the market clearing level. The higher wage will also induce more people to enter the labor market, creating a larger surplus of labor, making finding a job more competitive.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Efficiency wages cease to encourage work effort if all employers pay efficiency wages.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Employees will still be motivated to work hard even if all employers pay efficiency wages. This is because finding a new job if they get fired becomes more challenging, given that the higher wage across the industry means that one's marginal productivity must be higher to secure a job when the wage is above the market clearing level. Moreover, as the higher wage attracts more people to enter the labor market, there will be a larger surplus of labor, making job hunting more competitive."
  }

5.  Suppose the Russian government suddenly decides to privatize its oil fields.
True, False, and Explain:  If Russian voters suffer from debt aversion, we should expect their government's spending to increase. 
True, with caveats. Privatization in Russia has normally failed because the government sells industries to special interests, instead of those who will pay the most. If you assume they sell the industries to those who are willing to pay the most, and the future stream of payments from the industries are capitalized into the selling price, then we could expect this to occur. The Russian government, with this new revenue, can increase its spending without taking on more debt, and therefore without  angering debt-averse Russian voters who might otherwise prevent spending increases (assuming the government is responsive to voters, a la Wittman.)
Caplan seemed to think this was an adequate answer.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose the Russian government suddenly decides to privatize its oil fields. If Russian voters suffer from debt aversion, we should expect their government's spending to increase.",
    "answer": "True, with caveats.",
    "explanation": "Privatization in Russia has often been unsuccessful due to the government favoring special interests rather than the highest bidder. If we assume that the industries are sold to those willing to pay the most, with the future payments from the industries being capitalized into the selling price, then we could expect government spending to increase. This is because the Russian government would have new revenue, enabling it to spend more without taking on additional debt, thus not upsetting debt-averse voters who might otherwise oppose more spending. This also assumes the government is responsive to its voters."
  }

2. Build on Kahneman's chapter on "Answering an Easier Question" (Thinking, Fast and Slow) to explain a large belief gap between economists and the public on two questions from the Survey of Americans and Economists on the Economy.


---

[{"enunciate": "Explain a large belief gap between economists and the public on two questions from the Survey of Americans and Economists on the Economy, based on Kahneman's chapter on 'Answering an Easier Question' (Thinking, Fast and Slow).", "answer": "The belief gap between economists and the public may be due to individuals substituting complex questions with easier ones, resulting in different perspectives on the economy. This process often leads to differing perspectives, which may explain the belief gap between economists, who have more specialized knowledge of the economy, and the general public, who rely on more intuitive judgments. 3. All things considered, what is your best estimate of the true private return to education for the marginal 18 year old American? The true social return? Use empirics from class to ground your answer.
2013 FINAL EXAM


---

[{"enunciate": "All things considered, what is your best estimate of the true private return to education for the marginal 18-year-old American?", "answer": "Use empirics from class to ground your answer.", "explanation": "Please refer to the empirical data from the class to make an informed estimate of the true private return to education for the marginal 18-year-old American."}, {"enunciate": "What is the true social return?", "answer": "Use empirics from class to ground your answer.", "explanation": "Please refer to the empirical data from the class to make an informed estimate of the true social return to education."}]

***


1. Two players are deciding whether to contribute to a public good.  The public good is discrete: it is produced at the optimal level so long as one person contributes.  If the public good is produced, everyone gets a benefit of 3 if they didn't contribute, and 2 if they did.  If no one contributes, everyone gets 0.  
True, False, and Explain:  This is a Prisoners' Dilemma, not a Hawk-Dove game.
False. Like in a Hawk-Dove Game, there are two PSNE, both of which require that one person contributes while the other doesn't. Like in the hawk-dove games we saw in class, two PSNEs exist--both of which require one player chooses peace and the other player chooses war. Unlike the Hawk-Dove games we saw in class where peace, peace is a first best outcome, the contribute, contribute is not a first-best solution in the public goods provision game. This is not a prisoner's dilemma--a prisoner's dilemma has one PSNE, which is the first-best outcome.

*2007 Midterm -- similar question
Caplan said, The key feature of the Hawk-Dove game is that you if someone is doing something you want to do the opposite. So this IS a Hawk-Dove game.

    {
        "enunciate": "Two players are deciding whether to contribute to a public good. The public good is discrete: it is produced at the optimal level so long as one person contributes.         "answer": "False",
        "explanation": "This is not a Prisoners' Dilemma, but rather a Hawk-Dove game. There are two pure strategy Nash equilibria present, both requiring one player to contribute and the other not to contribute. The key feature of the Hawk-Dove game is that if someone is doing something, you want to do the opposite. 2. Suppose the demand curve for a contestable monopoly crosses the AC curve at three points.
True, False, and Explain:  There is a unique equilibrium.
 *Similar question 2005 Midterm
True[29].  Under contestable monopoly, P=AC, but only the higher quantity/lower price intersection is an equilibrium. At the higher intersection, even a monopolist that DID NOT face potential competition would want to cut price somewhat to earn additional profits. If you do face potential competition, you would have to cut your price all the way down to the lowest intersection of demand and AC to prevent entry.

At the higher intersection, even a monopolist that did not face potential competition would be motivated to reduce the price to gain additional profits. Facing potential competition, the monopolist would have to lower the price to the lowest intersection of demand and average cost to prevent entry."

3.  Suppose insurance companies are perfectly competitive and know true accident probabilities.  All of their potential customers are risk-neutral, but have a subjective P(getting in an accident) equal to the square root of the objective P(getting in an accident).

If, however, for all customers who face  0<Po<1 will buy insurance. In the situation that this is true for all customers, only then will all potential customers will definitely buy insurance. Because we cannot assume this to be the case, the answer is False. 
sqrt(P)>P. This means that all customers will overestimate their probability of getting into an accident.

4.  "Economic theory predicts that you are not enjoying this book as much as you thought you would." (Landsburg, The Armchair Economist)
True, False, and Explain:  As Landsburg shows, neoclassical economics is not only consistent with, but implies, violation of the rational expectations assumption.
False. The theory of a winner's curse relies on an assumption of rational expectations. Landsburg is describing a winner's curse here--your mean expectation regarding the quality of the book may be correct, but there is still a variance about the mean. One can be disappointed and have rational expectations: it is just the case that when you select a book you deem to be the best, there is more room for you to be disappointed. Rational expectations does not preclude imperfect information, rather it precludes systematic bias (your errors must be randomly distributed and average out to the mean.) 


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "\"Economic theory predicts that you are not enjoying this book as much as you thought you would.\" (Landsburg, The Armchair Economist) True, False, and Explain: As Landsburg shows, neoclassical economics is not only consistent with, but implies, violation of the rational expectations assumption.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Landsburg discusses the winner's curse, which relies on the assumption of rational expectations. In this case, disappointment may still occur even if the mean expectation about the book's quality is correct since there is still a variance around the mean. Rational expectations do not preclude imperfect information, but rather prevent systematic bias, meaning that errors need to be randomly distributed and average out to the mean. Therefore, one can be disappointed and still have rational expectations."
  }

5.  Suppose the entire return to education stems from signaling.  The signal, however, is noisy; some good workers don't have degrees, and some bad workers do. 
True, False, and Explain:  The measured return to education will fall to zero if you fully control for ALL of the traits that education signals.
?Returns?_i=??_0+?_1 ?education?_i+?_2 X_i^'+?_i?_
Signaling theory says that "people only want what the effort proves you already had in the first place." This means that if the entire return to education (higher earnings) stems from signaling, then those higher earnings derive entirely from the perceived underlying traits (ability, conscientiousness, etc) that education signals. If education perfectly signaled those traits, then controlling for ALL of those traits would cause ?_1to fall to 0. However, because the signal is noisy, some ?_1=? remains.
False.[30] Asymmetric information exists b/n employee and employer, so the employer must rely on the degree as a signal of "good worker traits". After controlling for ALL the traits that education signals,  the fact that some bad workers have degrees, means that the return to education is still positive. The fact that there is a return to education implies that employers do not have perfect information about the traits that education signals. Education is a noisy signal, though, some people without the underlying traits which are valued manage to get degrees. [31][32]The fact that education is a signal means that it has better than even odds to signal the underlying traits.

    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Since there is asymmetric information between the employee and employer, the employer must rely on the degree as a signal of 'good worker traits'. 6. Voters rational ignorance creates a moral hazard problem for politicians.
True, False, and Explain: As usual, there is a simple way to mitigate this moral hazard problem.
True. Voter rational ignorance means that voters don't obtain the information necessary to hold politicians accountable for their actions--i.e. voters face high monitoring costs. Thus politicians will not always act according to the interests of their constituents. This moral hazard problem could be overcome using contractual punishments, wherein voters and politicians would contract such that politicians voted into office would face high punishments if it is found that they acted against the interests of their constituents. This would provide a stronger incentive for politicians to act according to their constituents' interests rather than their own. 


---

    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "Voter rational ignorance refers to the situation where voters don't obtain the information necessary to hold politicians accountable for their actions, due to high monitoring costs. This leads to a moral hazard problem, as politicians may not always act in the best interests of their constituents."
  },
  {
    "enunciate": "Is there a simple way to mitigate this moral hazard problem? True or False?",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "One potential way to mitigate this moral hazard problem could be through contractual punishments between voters and politicians. By establishing contracts that impose high punishments on politicians who act against the interests of their constituents, a stronger incentive is created for politicians to prioritize constituent interests over their own."

1.) In the modern U.S., what is the most efficient way for the federal government to spend an extra billion dollars?  What is the maximally utilitarian way for the federal government to spend this sum?  (In both cases, assume that tax cuts are not an option).  Use everything you've learned to craft a thoughtful answer, and be specific
All situations are Pareto efficient because it is never possible to compensate the inevitable losers from a change due to transactions costs; I will therefore focus on Kaldor-Hicks efficiency, the maximization of the dollar value of social resources.
The government could maximize Kaldor-Hicks efficiency by having individuals bid for how they would like the money to be spent. By doing this, the government can ensure that the billion dollars will go towards the most highly valued use. (but if people can bid up to 1 billion dollars for a project, why won't they undertake the project themselves without the government?)
The goal under a utilitarian system is to maximize the total "happiness" or pleasure in a society. Usually, one thinks that this can be done by redistributing the billion dollars to those who are the poorest and have a higher marginal utility per dollar they receive. However, "utility monsters" may well exist, so the  government may maximize utility by spending it all on a project that one individual gains nearly infinite utility from, rather [33][34][35][36]than being concerned about everyone else. 
Two general observations:

    "answer": "The most efficient way is to maximize Kaldor-Hicks efficiency by having individuals bid for how they would like the money to be spent. The maximally utilitarian way is to either redistribute the billion dollars to the poorest with a higher marginal utility or to spend it all on a project that one individual gains nearly infinite utility from.",
    "explanation": "Under Kaldor-Hicks efficiency, the government could maximize the dollar value of social resources by allowing individuals to bid for the allocation of the extra billion dollars. This could be achieved by redistributing the funds to those who are poorest and have a higher marginal utility per dollar, or by spending the entire sum on a project that provides immense utility to a single individual."

1. When you claim that X is most efficient way for the federal government to spend an extra billion dollars, you should point to a large externality you propose to fix - and argue that the supply response will be substantial.  Answers of the form "Distribute the money equally to everyone" are weak, because all lump-sum transfers are neutral with respect to Kaldor-Hicks efficiency.

2. When you claim that X is the maximally utilitarian way for the federal government to spend an extra billion dollars, you should start with Kaldor-Hicks efficiency, then adjust for marginal utility of wealth.  The conventional adjustment is to put higher weight on the poor, but there are equally strong cases for putting higher weight on the appreciative (people who savor better experiences) and the materialistic (people who place unusually high value on the stuff that money can buy).  The true utilitarian will want to make each and every one of these adjustments.
By these standards, stand-out answers include:
Daniel Kuehn:
I agree with RPLong on a simple shape-of-utility curves basis that redistribution is the obvious first-cut winner (and do it progressively). Anything else needs to overcome normal consumer theory with an interesting externality or multiplier story.
I think investments in neglected R&D of any sort would be a strong contender. A billion goes a long way in a prize fund too.
Fabio Rojas:
Assumption #1: Economic growth happens when people innovate.
Assumption #2: Innovation is proportional to population size.
Policy prescription: Use the $1B to subsidize child bearing, such as lowering out of pocket costs for delivery, paying for child care, etc.
Why it's better than other policies: No other policy is simple to implement. Also, when it come to innovation, it has a better chance of working than having the state pick projects to subsidize.
Kaldor-Hicks criteria: Since the size of the economy will grow, we can use the additional tax revenue to compensate childless people without raising taxes.
Criticism: Maybe the elasticity of children and money (and the implied growth) isn't enough to offset the $1bn.
I'd say Fab understates the case, because Kaldor-Hicks would count additional people's willingness-to-pay to be born, and utilitarianism would count additional people's utility of existing.
Martin:
Pay Kim-Jong Un and his key (read: most influential) supporters to leave North Korea and never come back and send them to some undisclosed location (I am sure China would be willing to receive them).
Alternatively, pay-off any third world dictator and his key supporters to leave the country and never come back. 
But is a $1B payoff really sufficient to accomplish this?  And wouldn't the dictators reasonably fear a post-abdication clawback?
AMW proposes immigration subsidies.  But unless you relax immigration laws at the same time, would these subsidies really accomplish anything?  The same goes for Bob Murphy's righteous suggestion.
Ryan Murphy:
Endow a fund that spends 5% of the endowment per year on prizes for solving specific scientific problems.
A very good start, but you should specify "specific scientific problems" with especially large externalities (for maximum efficiency) and especially large effects on the poor, appreciative, and materialistic (for maximum utility).
There are several enticing proposals to spend the money improving public opinion on e.g. immigration.  But how much difference would $1B of open borders propaganda really make on policy?
Thomas May makes a good case:
Sending medicine and vaccinations to the third world is probably the way to provide the greatest happiness to the greatest number for the lowest cost. The domestic welfare state by and large isn't because people in the US tend to have a lot of money by world standards anyway, and development aid (as opposed to relief of extreme hardship) isn't either because it doesn't seem to do much of anything.

2.) Some people argue that nominal wage rigidity is individually rational because many people (especially home-owners) have large nominally-denominated debt.  Is this a sound argument?  Why or why not?
The theory of nominal wage rigidity deals with how the labor market responds to changes, or lack there of, in wages. People prefer to have rigid nominal wages because of fairness concerns, and concerns that if wages fall worker morale and productivity will also fall. It does not deal with nominally-denominated debt. The only concern related to debt is that if nominal wages are rigid during a recession, then there is an increased risk of unemployment for the average worker[37][38][39], and if he is unemployed he cannot pay back his debt.
This argument may hold if we are discussing downward wage rigidity. A fall in nominal wages would result in the price level falling, so as to keep the real wage constant. If a person's mortgage contract is in nominally-denominated debt, their debt burden will actually be now be higher in real terms. However, we may expect individuals to support nominal wage increases, which will increase the price level, so as to inflate their debt away. As the price level rises, and each dollar can command fewer goods, their real debt burden falls. In this case, the argument above would not be sound, as home-owners may support nominal wage increases.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Some people argue that nominal wage rigidity is individually rational because many people (especially home-owners) have large nominally-denominated debt. Is this a sound argument? Why or why not?",
    "answer": "This argument may hold if we are discussing downward wage rigidity, but not if we are discussing nominal wage increases.",
    "explanation": "The theory of nominal wage rigidity deals with how the labor market responds to changes or lack thereof in wages, not specifically with nominally-denominated debt. If there is downward wage rigidity, a fall in nominal wages would result in the price level falling to keep the real wage constant. This could increase the real debt burden for people with nominally-denominated debt, like home-owners. However, if we consider nominal wage increases, which will raise the price level and potentially inflate debt away, the argument would not be sound, as home-owners may support nominal wage increases to reduce their real debt burden."
  }
]

3.) Paul Krugman recently accused anti-Keynesians of being "knaves and fools."  What would Kahneman say about this accusation?  Use Thinking, Fast and Slow  to craft a thoughtful reply.
The halo effect may play a role here, with Krugman reaching the conclusion that people are "knaves and fools" because they hold an opinion on economic science that he believes is mistaken. The halo effect occurs when people develop a unified view of a person or entity to create a coherent narrative. So one negative thing about someone (such as a mistaken opinion on economics) will support a broader narrative of them as a fool or a knave. 
Krugman may also be suffering from an availability bias--it could be that Krugman can easily name many anti-Keynesians who have acted in what he perceives to be a foolish or knavish manner, and this bias causes him to evaluate anti-Keynesians as knaves or fools. It is true that experts are less likely to to be affected by ease of retrieval, but those who are powerful (which Krugman most certainly is in the intellectual landscape) are MORE likely to do this.
Also confirmation bias. Krugman is more likely to seek out information that confirms his pre-existing beliefs, preventing him from updating properly. 
2014 FINAL EXAM


---

[{"enunciate": "Paul Krugman recently accused anti-Keynesians of being \"knaves and fools.\" What would Kahneman say about this accusation? Use Thinking, Fast and Slow to craft a thoughtful reply.", "answer": "The halo effect, availability bias, and confirmation bias may all contribute to Krugman's accusation.", "explanation": "Kahneman might suggest that Krugman's accusation could be influenced by the halo effect, availability bias, and confirmation bias. The halo effect occurs when people create a unified view of a person or entity based on one negative aspect, such as a mistaken opinion on economics, leading to the perception of someone being a fool or knave. Availability bias may also play a role if Krugman can easily recall examples of anti-Keynesians behaving foolishly or knavishly, causing him to evaluate them as such. Although experts are less likely to be affected by ease of retrieval, those in positions of power, like Krugman, are more likely to be influenced by this bias. Additionally, confirmation bias might affect Krugman, as he is more likely to seek out information confirming his pre-existing beliefs and resist updating his views."}]

1. Some economists worry that economies will become increasingly unequal if the real interest rate r exceeds the rate of economic growth g.
True, False, and Explain:  In an intertemporal general equilibrium consumption model, the real interest rate r cannot exceed the rate of economic growth g.
Week 2 notes, Section VIII, F: "interest rates in general equilibrium must rise high enough that people are content consumering their current endowment and no more. This happens when (1+r) = (1+g)/?. 
If beta = 1, then r must equal g. 
False, If beta <1, then r>g. See proof: 
Proof, given that ?<1.
(1+r)=(1+g)/?
?=(1+g)/(1+r)
1>?=(1+g)/(1+r)
1>(1+g)/(1+r)
1+r>1+g
r>g

[{"enuncate": "Some economists worry that economies will become increasingly unequal if the real interest rate r exceeds the rate of economic growth g. True, False, and Explain: In an intertemporal general equilibrium consumption model, the real interest rate r cannot exceed the rate of economic growth g.", "answer": "False", "explanation": "In an intertemporal general equilibrium consumption model, the real interest rate r can exceed the rate of economic growth g if beta is less than 1. This is because interest rates in general equilibrium must rise high enough that people are content consuming their current endowment and no more. When beta is equal to 1, r must equal g. However, if beta is less than 1, then r can be greater than g. This is proved by the following inequality: 1 > (1+g)/(1+r) -> 1+r > 1+g -> r > g."}]

False, while you do not need communication to reach a PSNE in a Coordination game, it is not like a PD game. Specifically, a PD has only one PSNE while a Coordination game has two. In a coordination game, two players can coordinate using focal points like culture and location. However, communication increases the likelihood  that the players will reach a PSNE, and one with the higher valued payoff. In a PD game, there is only one PSNE: defect, defect. Communication is not necessary for players to reach this outcome; instead, it may move players away from it.

3.  An agent faces two choices: (1) A utility of 10 for sure; (2) A utility of 0 with 50% probability and a utility of 20 with 50% probability.
True, False, and Explain: If the agent is indifferent between (1) and (2), then in terms of Expected Utility Theory, he must be risk-neutral.
False. Expected Utility Theory concerns utility derived from wealth and other consumer goods, and says that an agent will seek to maximize that utility. The question gives the output of the utility function, so the level of risk-aversion is already accounted for. Thus, no matter what the agent's level of risk-aversion is, he/ she is indifferent between (1) and (2).

4.  "No one really has preferences for fair or vindictive behavior. When people act Ôfairly' or Ôvindictively,' they're just signaling."  
True, False, and Explain:  This claim is not theoretically consistent with the signaling model.
False. Signaling theory says that people will incur non-productive cost in order to send a signal that gives them an advantage in the market by indicating what "type" they are over those who will not and/or cannot incur the costs necessary to send that signal.  Acting fairly or vindictively requires that someone incur costs that do not directly lead to benefit, assuming people do not have preferences for fair or vindictive behavior. When someone acts fairly or vindictively, they hope to gain from that behavior in future interactions--if you think I am of the "fair" type, you will be more likely to deal with me, and if I am of the "vindictive" type, you will be less likely to cross me. While the signaling model does not imply that people are signaling when they act fairly or vindictively, it is compatible with the signaling model for these behaviors to be entirely signaling.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "\"No one really has preferences for fair or vindictive behavior. When people act fairly or vindictively, they're just signaling.\" True, False, and Explain: This claim is not theoretically consistent with the signaling model.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "According to signaling theory, individuals may incur non-productive costs to send signals that provide them with a competitive advantage by indicating their behavioral 'type'. Acting fairly or vindictively involves incurring costs without direct benefits, assuming people don't have innate preferences for these behaviors. The expectation is that these actions will lead to advantageous outcomes in future interactions. Being perceived as 'fair' might make others more likely to interact with you, while being seen as 'vindictive' might prevent people from crossing you. Although the signaling model doesn't imply that people are always signaling when they act fairly or vindictively, it is compatible with the idea that these behaviors could be solely motivated by signaling."
  }
]

5.  "The initial attempt to believe is an automatic operation of System 1, which involves the construction of the best possible interpretation of the situation." (Kahneman, Thinking, Fast and Slow) (p. 81)
True, False, and Explain:  This model of belief formation implies that lies are persuasive because people are usually telling the truth. 
False. Here is the quote with context (pg. 81):
"Gilbert proposed that understanding a statement must begin with an attempt to believe it: you must first know what the idea would mean if it were true. Only then can you decide whether or not to unbelieve it. The initial attempt to believe is an automatic operation of System 1, which involves a construction of the best possible interpretation of the situation. Even a nonsensical statement, Gilbert argues, will evoke initial belief."
False, this model doesn't rely on an assumption that people are usually truthful; rather, it relies on testing the implications of the statement in order to determine its validity. Kahneman says that System 1 accepts the statement as true so that System 2 can evaluate how its implications match reality.


---

{"questions": [
  {
    "enunciate": "This model of belief formation implies that lies are persuasive because people are usually telling the truth.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "This model of belief formation, as proposed by Gilbert, does not rely on an assumption that people are usually truthful. Instead, it requires first accepting the statement as true, allowing System 1 to construct the best possible interpretation, and then testing its implications to determine its validity, which is the job of System 2."
  }

6. Senior #1 and Senior #2 are both undergraduates in their final year of college.  Senior #1 knows he has a 90% chance of successfully graduating this year.  Senior #2 knows he has a 90% chance of failing out.  Both students satisfy the Permanent Income Hypothesis.
True, False, and Explain: Both students will increase their consumption if they successfully graduate, but Senior #1 will increase his post-graduation consumption by a smaller fraction than Senior #2.
True. After graduation (or when the chance of graduating increases  from their prior expectation to 100%), both students will increase their expected permanent income. Senior #1 will have a smaller change in expected permanent income than Senior #2. Since both satisfy the Permanent Income Hypothesis, they will have smoothed consumption according to their expected permanent income. Since Senior #1 has a smaller change in expected income, his consumption will change by a smaller fraction than Senior #2

Senior #2 knows he has a 90% chance of failing out. Both students satisfy the Permanent Income Hypothesis. True, False, and Explain: Both students will increase their consumption if they successfully graduate, but Senior #1 will increase his post-graduation consumption by a smaller fraction than Senior #2.", "answer": "True", "explanation": "After graduation (or when the chance of graduating increases from their prior expectation to 100%), both students will increase their expected permanent income. Senior #1 will have a smaller change in expected permanent income than Senior #2. Since both satisfy the Permanent Income Hypothesis, they will have smoothed consumption according to their expected permanent income. Since Senior #1 has a smaller change in expected income, his consumption will change by a smaller fraction than Senior #2."}]

1.) In the real world, what would happen if a restaurant adopted Landsburg's system for splitting checks? (The Armchair Economist)
Landsburg (p. 31-32) says that everyone should pay the whole bill. If a restaurant were to implement this system, then the restaurant would start pay customers to come in (whether through bribes, coupons, etc), up to the point where the excess profits they would earn are being exhausted. Ultimately, people would still end bearing the full costs of what they purchased, as they are no longer able to free ride and push the costs onto other diners when the bill is split.
However, in the real world, if a restaurant, say restaurant A, were to implement this system, it would likely go out of business. Assuming that no other restaurant implements Landsburg's system, fewer people would go to restaurant A because they can free ride on the rest of the dinner party at restaurant B, where they split the check evenly between the group. Additionally, some people may prefer to split checks rather than deal with a new convoluted system. In the real world with the advent of computers, splitting checks has become much easier, so the assumption in Landsburg's story that the check cannot be split is unlikely to hold.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "In the real world, what would happen if a restaurant adopted Landsburg's system for splitting checks?",
    "answer": "The restaurant would likely go out of business.",
    "explanation": "Landsburg suggests that everyone should pay the whole bill, and if a restaurant implemented this system, they would try to attract customers with incentives like bribes or coupons. However, in the real world, customers would prefer going to other restaurants where they can split the check and free ride on the rest of the dinner party. Moreover, with the advent of computers, splitting checks has become much easier, making the assumption in Landsburg's story that checks cannot be split unlikely to hold. As a result, the restaurant adopting Landsburg's system would likely go out of business due to a decrease in customers."
  }
]

2.) Use the signaling model of education to explain the sheepskin effect. Precisely specify why, in the real world, discrete educational achievement is so much more informative to employers than continuous educational achievement.
The sheepskin effect describes the fact that the vast majority of the returns to education are achieved upon completing the final year and graduating. This is consistent with the signaling model, which says that education is mostly about showing that you can make it through school and incur costs that others cannot, revealing to potential employers what "type" you are. 
In the real world, discrete educational achievement is more important than continuous educational achievement because schools do not impart many skills to you--the human capital effects of education are quite small. We can see this happening in the real world--if skills really mattered, I could theoretically move to New Jersey, attend classes at Princeton (the greatest university the world has ever known) without matriculating, and gain the skills from those courses to increase my salary. However, employers don't care if you've attended the courses, only if you have obtained the degree. Discrete educational achievement is more informative because it sends the signal (the diploma) that you can bear the full costs of your schooling, providing employers with more information about your "type" and enhancing your employment prospects.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Use the signaling model of education to explain the sheepskin effect. Precisely specify why, in the real world, discrete educational achievement is so much more informative to employers than continuous educational achievement.",
    "answer": "The sheepskin effect describes the fact that the vast majority of the returns to education are achieved upon completing the final year and graduating. In the real world, discrete educational achievement is more important than continuous educational achievement because schools do not impart many skills and the human capital effects of education are quite small. Discrete educational achievement is more informative because it sends the signal (the diploma) that an individual can bear the full costs of their schooling, providing employers with more information about their 'type' and enhancing their employment prospects.",
    "explanation": "The sheepskin effect is in line with the signaling model, suggesting that education predominantly serves as a signal to potential employers about an individual's ability to complete school and endure costs that others may not be able to handle. This reveals their 'type' or abilities to employers. Discrete educational achievements, such as obtaining a degree, carry more weight than continuous achievements because they indicate that a person has fully committed to their education and successfully completed it. This provides valuable information to employers about the individual's commitment, perseverance, and ability to handle challenges. In the real world, employers tend to value degrees and graduation over simply attending courses since it is a stronger signal of a person's capabilities."
  }

2. Suppose all firms in an industry have the same cost function TC=a+bq.
True, False, and Explain:  Contestable monopoly and Bertrand competition both lead to "first-best" efficient outcomes if a=0, and "second-best" efficient outcomes if a>0.
*a = fixed costs* 
 *2007 Midterm -- similar question
True. If there are zero fixed costs (a=0)  and the most efficient firm faces an equally efficient competitor, both yield the first-best efficient outcome of P=MC. If there are fixed costs(a>0) and the most efficient firm faces an equally efficient competitor, both yield the second-best efficient outcome of P=AC. If the most efficient firm does not face an equally efficient competitor, then price can exceed both MC and AC. In this case, however, the firms are equally efficient because they have the same cost function, so this will not pose a problem.


---

    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "If there are zero fixed costs (a=0) and the most efficient firm faces an equally efficient competitor, both yield the first-best efficient outcome of P=MC. If there are fixed costs (a>0) and the most efficient firm faces an equally efficient competitor, both yield the second-best efficient outcome of P=AC. If the most efficient firm does not face an equally efficient competitor, then price can exceed both MC and AC. However, in this case, the firms are equally efficient because they have the same cost function, so this will not pose a problem."

price of insurance = (probability of receiving insurance)*(rate of insurance)
	rate of insurance = X
	price of insurance = P(insurance)*X*i
Maxi 0.5(10,000 - 0.75i)0.5 + 0.5(0 + i - 0.75i)0.5
Maxi 0.5(10,000 - 0.75i)0.5 + 0.5(0.25i)0.5
d/di=(0.5)(0.5)(-0.75)?(10,000-0.75i)?^(-0.5)+(0.5)(0.5)(0.25)?(0.25i)?^(-0.5)=0
	(0.5)(0.5)(0.25)?(0.25i)?^(-0.5)=(0.5)(0.5)(0.75)?(10,000-0.75i)?^(-0.5)
	?(0.25i)?^(-0.5)=3?(10,000-0.75i)?^(-0.5)
	0.25i=1/9(10,000-0.75i)
	1/4 i=10,000/9-1/12 i
	1/3 i=10,000/9
	i^*=10,000/3Å$3,333.33>$1,000

5.  True, False, and Explain:  Paying efficiency wages is a Prisoners' Dilemma, because it's impossible for all employers to pay "above-market" wages. 
False, it is not impossible for all employers to pay Ôabove-market' wages. Above-market in this case does not mean above what your competitors are paying; it means above the market clearing wage. In fact, employers want to raise wages above the Ôabove-market' wage in order to encourage workers to shirk less and make the threat of being fired more serious, given that the pool of unemployed workers will now be larger and the higher wages make it more difficult for them to procure employment. If all employers pay the efficiency wage it will create a permanent labor surplus which will further increase the costs for a worker if he/she is fired. (draw graph?)


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Paying efficiency wages is a Prisoners' Dilemma, because it's impossible for all employers to pay 'above-market' wages.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "It is not impossible for all employers to pay 'above-market' wages. In this context, 'above-market' does not mean higher than what competitors are paying; it refers to wages above the market-clearing rate. Employers may raise wages above this rate to encourage workers to shirk less and make the threat of being fired more serious, given the larger pool of unemployed workers and the higher wages making it harder for them to find new employment. If all employers pay the efficiency wage, it will create a permanent labor surplus, further increasing the costs for a worker if they are fired."

6. Financial analysts often appeal to "herd behavior" to explain price movements in financial markets: Investors want to buy stocks that have been increasing in price, and sell stocks that have been decreasing in price.
True, False, and Explain: According to Thaler (The Winner's Curse), herd behavior is not, on average, a profitable strategy for individual investors.
*(See Chapter 12) p. 157: "Our own research on this topic (De Bondt and Thaler, 1985, 1987) was motivated by the hypothesis that the contrarian strategies are successful because of systematic investor overreaction. There is substantial evidence in the psychology literature that individuals tend to overweight recent data in making forecasts and judgments"
False, Thaler doesn't discuss herd behavior in Winner's Curse. Instead, his research finds that systematic investor overreaction leads to mean reversion in the stock market. It is possible that this overreaction is not because of herd behavior--instead, individuals are over-reacting based on information from previous periods independently. Thus, the profitable strategy is to buy those stocks that have been doing poorly and sell those that have been doing well.
The Q&A with Caplan revealed that this statement is true - herd behavior is not a profitable strategy on average. Rather, one can make money as a contrarian investor, taking into account mean reversion.
Part 2: Short Answer


---

    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Thaler's research in The Winner's Curse does not focus on herd behavior. Instead, it suggests that systematic investor overreaction leads to mean reversion in the stock market. This overreaction may not be due to herd behavior, but rather individuals over-reacting based on information from previous periods independently. A profitable strategy would be to buy stocks that have been performing poorly and sell those that have been performing well. Herd behavior is not considered a profitable strategy on average."

1. Suppose a pirate robs ten people and puts all of their money inside a treasure chest.  When the police recover the chest (and before the pirate can spend a penny), they ask each of the ten victims to state how much money he lost.  To discourage lying, the police announce that if the total losses claimed by the victims exceeds the total amount of money in the chest, none of the victims will get any money back.  Using everything you've learned, what would actually happen in this scenario?  Justify your answer.
In this case, the game theoretic prediction would be for everyone to declare the exact amount of money they lost, therefore ensuring that the total amounts claimed would equal the amount in the chest. In this game, there are multiple PSNE - people can claim any amount, so long as the totals add up to the amount in the chest. However, everyone claiming their exact amount will likely act as a focal point, as each individual will expect the others to claim their exact amounts so as to maximize their take, and thus you will as well. In reality, I may expect the amount to be lower. Kahneman introduces the cost of loss aversion - that people weight losses of an amount higher than the gains - so I would expect individuals to claim slightly less than they had lost so as to ensure they do not go home empty handed.

What would actually happen in this scenario? Justify your answer.",

2. Both the human capital and signaling models of education suggest that online education will expand more quickly if Ôearly-adopters' of online education are relatively strong students."  Do you agree?  Carefully explain your reasoning.
To answer this question, we would need to know more about the structure of the online education. For the human capital model, it depends on if the online education resources allow students to work together. The presence of other strong students may serve to help you build better human capital, as you have the opportunity to form study groups or learn from other talented students. If the structure does not allow you to interact with smarter students but instead has you working alone, we should not expect the early adopters being strong students to have any effects. For the signaling model, the online education must be "costly" enough to serve as an effective signal - there must be some barriers to completing. If this is the case, then talented students would increase the adoption of online education - their presences would increase the strength of the signal (talented students come from this program.) If this is true, we would expect others to rapidly adopt the model so as to signal to employers they are higher ability, and thus deserving of higher wages.

    "answer": "It depends on the structure of the online education and if it allows students to work together or has barriers to completing.",
    "explanation": "In the human capital model, the expansion of online education depends on whether it allows students to work together. Having strong early adopters could help build better human capital if they can form study groups or learn from each other. In the signaling model, the online education must be costly enough to serve as an effective signal. If strong early adopters increase the strength of the signal, then others might rapidly adopt the model to signal their higher ability and deserve higher wages. 1. In a world with no externalities, suppose the government imposes a lump sum tax and uses it to fund an hourly work subsidy. 
True, False, and Explain:  The deadweight cost of this tax-and-subsidy policy is zero.
False. It is true that the DWL of a lump sum tax is zero. However, assuming the market starts out in equilibrium, a subsidy carries a DWL because it distorts the allocation of resources.  There would be a loss in both consumer and producer surplus.  Supply Shifts out beyond the natural equilibrium level because you are trying to increase the labor supply. 
Caplan seemed to like this answer. He said that this would cause labor to be allocated to lower valued uses. And a lump sum tax to pay for wage subsidies to deal with efficiency wage unemployment doesn't make sense because you'd be taxing the unemployed and then subsidizing them to do a lower-valued job.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "In a world with no externalities, suppose the government imposes a lump sum tax and uses it to fund an hourly work subsidy. Is the deadweight cost of this tax-and-subsidy policy zero?",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "While it is true that the deadweight loss (DWL) of a lump sum tax is zero, a subsidy can still create a DWL if it distorts the allocation of resources. In this case, the market may start in equilibrium, but a labor subsidy might shift the supply beyond the natural equilibrium level. This could lead to a loss in both consumer and producer surplus, as labor is allocated to less valuable uses. Additionally, taxing the unemployed and then subsidizing them to do lower-valued jobs is not an efficient approach to address wage unemployment."
  }

3.  True, False, and Explain: In the real world, risk misperceptions unambiguously reduce the demand for insurance.
False. As Kahneman documents, people tend to either overestimate the risk of low-probability events or treat them as if they have a zero probability. This overestimation could lead individuals to over-insure. Moreover, individuals who are more risk-averse are more likely to buy insurance, but they are also less likely to engage in risky behaviors that make such insurance a good investment for the buyer. Risk-misperceptions may therefore increase demand for insurance rather than just decreasing it. [41]

This overestimation could lead individuals to over-insure. Furthermore, individuals who are more risk-averse are more likely to purchase insurance, but they are also less likely to engage in risky behaviors that make such insurance a good investment for the buyer. Therefore, risk misperceptions may increase demand for insurance, rather than just decreasing it."

4. "As in the judgment of whether a work of art is genuine or a fake, you will usually do better by focusing on its provenance than by looking at the piece itself." (Kahneman, Thinking, Fast and Slow)
True, False, and Explain:  Kahneman is arguing that elite credentials are the best predictor of expert accuracy.
False. On page 243, Kahneman says this and follows it with "If the environment is sufficiently regular and if the judge has had a chance to learn its regularities, the associative machinery will recognize situations and generate quick and accurate predictions and decisions. You can trust someone's intuitions if these conditions are met."
Kahneman is not arguing that elite credentials are the best predictor of expert accuracy. Instead, experts are likely to have accurate intuition if they have experience in an environment with strong regularities that they have built experience with.  For example, firefighters have experience in environments with regularities that they develop intuitions and memories about. Political scientists do not have similar experiences or regularities with regards to elections.

5. Suppose workers are paid nominally rigid efficiency wages. 
True, False, and Explain:  Employee shirking will be procyclical  -  i.e., higher during good economic times, lower during bad economic times.
UPGRADE THIS ANSWER AFTER WE HAVE LEARNED THIS IN CLASS
This appears to be true.  Wage rigidity implies that it will be less costly to fire a given worker and hire a new worker than to lower a worker's wage if that worker's productivity falls. This is likely to be particularly true in a recession when there are more unemployed workers seeking jobs. Moreover, during a recession a worker who is fired for shirking will find it more difficult to find a job, because fewer firms will be hiring. The fired worker will also be unable to compete on price, because this will signal a lower quality worker to employers assuming the nominally rigid efficiency wages result from asymmetric information. All of this suggests that during recessions the expected cost to an employee of shirking is higher than during good economic times. Employee shirking will therefore be procyclical.


---

True, False, and Explain: Employee shirking will be procyclical - i.e., higher during good economic times, lower during bad economic times.", "answer": "True", "explanation": "Wage rigidity implies that it will be less costly to fire a given worker and hire a new worker than to lower a worker's wage if that worker's productivity falls. 1.  "Moral hazard is at least second-best efficient because there's really nothing government can do to fix it."  Using everything you've learned, critically analyze this statement.
- Second-best efficient: moral hazard is second best efficient when the marginal costs of eliminating it exceed the benefits of doing so
    - so, moral hazard is second-best efficient in the Kaldor-Hick sense
- Statement is not necessarily true: 
- punitive contracts: "if the less-informed can pay to observe the more-informed , then they can enforce good behavior at a lwo cost with random monitoring and threats of severe punishment." 
    - government doesn't allow for these contracts meaning that they eliminate the tools businesses have to eliminate moral hazard
           - insurance
           - monitoring people on the job: how much time at work do they actually spend working
- Jersey drivers
    - government actually subsidizes   bad drivers, increasing the moral hazard
It is not evident that "moral hazard is at least second-best efficient because there's really nothing government can do to fix it." Government regulations may exacerbate moral hazard - for example, by limiting the punishment companies can levy on individuals who break contracts, companies are deprived of a tool to make the costs of moral hazard higher and thus reduce it. In addition, government subsidies - such as those for risky drivers in New Jersey - can make matters worse by creating moral hazard problems for risky drivers. Government can also do some things like increase the penalties for insurance fraud. Moral hazard is second best efficient whenever the marginal costs of monitoring are higher than the marginal benefits of doing so. In this case, accepting some moral hazard maximizes efficiency in the Kaldor-Hicks sense.  [42]


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Moral hazard is at least second-best efficient because there's really nothing government can do to fix it. Using everything you've learned, critically analyze this statement.",
    "answer": "It is not evident that moral hazard is at least second-best efficient because there's really nothing government can do to fix it. Government regulations may exacerbate moral hazard and government can also do some things like increase the penalties for insurance fraud.",
    "explanation": "The statement that moral hazard is at least second-best efficient because there's really nothing government can do to fix it is not necessarily true. Government regulations can inadvertently increase moral hazard, such as by limiting the punishment companies can impose on individuals who break contracts. This deprivation of a tool to make the costs of moral hazard higher could reduce it. Additionally, government subsidies, like the ones for risky drivers in New Jersey, can exacerbate the problem by creating moral hazard for these drivers. Government can take actions, such as increasing penalties for insurance fraud, to combat moral hazard. Moral hazard is second best efficient when the marginal costs of monitoring are higher than the marginal benefits, which maximizes efficiency in the Kaldor-Hicks sense."
  }
]

2. How will you revise your long-term financial plans in light of the evidence from behavioral finance?  Methodically explain your answer.
- save more
- take income over 9 months
- withholdings

Q.  In a world with no externalities, suppose the government imposes a lump sum tax and uses it to fund an hourly work subsidy. 
True, False, and Explain:  The deadweight cost of this tax-and-subsidy policy is zero.
False. It is true that the DWL of a lump sum tax is zero. However, assuming the market starts out in equilibrium, a subsidy carries a DWL because it distorts the allocation of resources.  There would be a loss in both consumer and producer surplus. 
Supply Shifts out beyond the natural equilibrium level because you are trying to increase the labor supply.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "In a world with no externalities, suppose the government imposes a lump sum tax and uses it to fund an hourly work subsidy. True, False, and Explain: The deadweight cost of this tax-and-subsidy policy is zero.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "While it is true that the deadweight loss of a lump sum tax is zero, a subsidy creates a deadweight loss since it distorts the allocation of resources when the market is initially in equilibrium. This leads to a decrease in both consumer and producer surplus, as the supply shifts beyond the natural equilibrium level due to an attempt to increase the labor supply."
  }
]

Q. True, False, and Explain:  In the real world, lump-sum taxes are always the first-best efficient way for governments to raise a given amount of revenue.
False.  While an economic model suggests the truth of that statement, given that any other form of tax results in a Dead Weight Loss, this comment is false in the real world. The lump-sum tax model avoids DWL and allocative inefficiency, imposing no additional costs from changes in behavior. However, in the real world, Lump-sum taxes are seen as regressive.  Thus, Lump-sum taxes are also a politically impossible and are not a first-best solution


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: In the real world, lump-sum taxes are always the first-best efficient way for governments to raise a given amount of revenue.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Although economic models may suggest that lump-sum taxes are the most efficient way to raise revenue due to the absence of deadweight loss and allocative inefficiency, in reality, they are considered regressive and politically unfeasible. Therefore, lump-sum taxes are not the first-best solution in the real world."
  }

From an Economic Stance the Lump Sum is Good, but from a political stance this is not so.  This is because there is no DWL. However, this is not a political reality because they are considered highly regressive.  From a politicians' perspective, they raise debt, inflation to create constituent spending. Tarriffs and quotas are also in the politician's repertoire because they lack salience to the consumer as a tax.  Finally, Politicians prefer a tax on products with negative externalities.

Q. Suppose there are an infinite number of equally efficient potential producers.
True, False, and Explain: With constant MC and zero fixed costs, there are an infinite number of PSNE and an infinite number of MSNE.
TRUE.  We can think of this as an Entry game, with an infinite number of firms choosing in or out.  A PSNE would be an individual firm's choice of entry.  With an infinite number of firms, there are an infinite number of PSNE combinations. Regarding MSNEs, with no profits to be made, a firm would face n-dimensional payoff matrix in which firms are indifferent between entry and absence.  With an infinite number of firms, each one having an MSNE, there would thus be an infinite number of MSNEs


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose there are an infinite number of equally efficient potential producers. True, False, and Explain: With constant MC and zero fixed costs, there are an infinite number of PSNE and an infinite number of MSNE.",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "This scenario can be viewed as an Entry game, where an infinite number of firms decide whether to enter or stay out of the market. A PSNE is an individual firm's decision to enter. Since there are infinite firms, there are infinite PSNE combinations. Regarding MSNEs, if there are no profits to be made, firms would face an n-dimensional payoff matrix where they are indifferent between entry and absence. Hence, with an infinite number of firms, each having an MSNE, there would be an infinite number of MSNEs."
  }
]

Right
Left
1,5
0,0
Right
0,0
5,1
True, False, and Explain: What Kreps calls "cheap talk" cannot improve the efficiency of this game if both players engage in backwards induction.
False.  It can if they are acting with imperfect information, and if they are acting simultaneously.  With this set of circumstance, you don't know what the other will do.  If we allow for the possibility that you are using induction to predict probabilities with simultaneous action, cheap talk could improve efficiency.  Cheap talk, would then change the probability of having a 0,0 outcome in the first round. 
True. If they are already engaging in backwards induction they can't improve upon their optimums by simply talking about it.  Backward induction would lead to pareto efficient optimums.  With talk, they would agree to alternating turns. This is also pareto efficient, as the aggregate payoff is the same.  That is, the idea of cheap talk allows players to ascertain better mutual benefit, so long as there aren't dominated strategies. Thus, they would simply reach the efficiency in any case.  Same number of PSNE and sub-game perfect with both extensive and normal forms.

    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "If players are acting with imperfect information and simultaneously, cheap talk could improve efficiency. Q. Suppose two people repeatedly play the Dictator game.  Player 1 gets to be the Dictator in odd-numbered turns; Player 2 gets to be the Dictator in even-numbered turns.  ?²1 for both players.  After each turn, the game ends with 99% probability.
True, False, and Explain: An equilibrium where the Dictator always offers even splits (50/50) may be sustainable if the stakes increase over time.
True. Even with a 99% Probability that the game ends after every turn, it still qualifies as an infinitely repeating game, in terms of the player strategies.  Given that the stakes are increasing in every turn, so long as a person is sufficiently risk tolerant, they may continue to offer equitable splits in the hope of reciprocity in the next turn.

Q. Consider a simple auction.
True, False, and Explain:  As long as actors have RE, the Winner's Curse will not cause the market to "unravel."
True.  With or without rational expectations, this statement is valid since the winner's curse doesn't lead to the market unraveling but simply to the winner suffering a loss.  Auction markets in which the winner's curse is frequently observed continue, as other players don't experience the loss.

    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "The statement is valid with or without rational expectations. The winner's curse doesn't cause the market to unravel, but rather leads to the winner experiencing a loss. Auction markets where the winner's curse is often observed still continue, as other participants don't experience this loss."

Q.  Historians often argue that wars arise due to irrationality, not just bad luck.  What simple game theory model are historians implicitly rejecting?  How could historians use Thaler's The Winner's Curse to persuasively defend their position?  Be specific.
Historians are ignoring the Hawk-Dove Game, in which one player, by choosing combative behavior when the other player chooses peace behavior, elicits the highest possible payoff. Thus, so long as the actor assumes that the other player will act peaceably, he is acting rationally. 
Historians, however, could reasonably defend their positions by arguing that wars often turn out to be costlier than expected.  In this case, initiators are subject to the high bids, as in the case of the Winner's Curse.  As a result, though they may be accurate in believing that they will emerge victorious, they will have expended more from the fight than the spoils are worth.  [Example]


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "What simple game theory model are historians implicitly rejecting? How could historians use Thaler's The Winner's Curse to persuasively defend their position? Be specific.",
    "answer": "Historians are ignoring the Hawk-Dove Game. They could use Thaler's The Winner's Curse to defend their position by arguing that wars often turn out to be costlier than expected, and initiators are subject to high bids like in the Winner's Curse.",
    "explanation": "Historians often overlook the Hawk-Dove Game, where one player achieves the highest payoff by choosing combative behavior while the other player chooses peaceful behavior. This suggests that actors are rational if they assume the other player will act peacefully. Yet, historians can argue their stance by suggesting that wars frequently end up costing more than anticipated, making initiators experience high bids similar to the Winner's Curse. Consequently, even if they believe they'll win, they may spend more on the conflict than the rewards justify."
  }
]

Q. "Consider two professors.  John earns $55,000 paid in monthly installments.  Joan earns a base salary of $45,000 paid over twelve months, and a guaranteed extra $10,000 paid during the summer months." (Thaler, The Winner's Curse)
True, False, and Explain:  Thaler explains that the "mental accounting" prediction is that Joan will outspend John during the summer, but John will outspend Joan throughout the rest of the year.
FALSE, Mental accounting predicts that individuals segregate gains and integrate loses. Thus, Joan, who suddenly  receives higher income during the summer months, will likely save the surplus treating it as an asset.  John, who earns a steady income, is more likely to adhere to a standard consumption-smottihing behavior.


---

[{"enunciate": "Consider two professors. John earns $55,000 paid in monthly installments. Joan earns a base salary of $45,000 paid over twelve months, and a guaranteed extra $10,000 paid during the summer months. True, False, and Explain: Thaler explains that the \"mental accounting\" prediction is that Joan will outspend John during the summer, but John will outspend Joan throughout the rest of the year.", "answer": "FALSE", "explanation": "Mental accounting predicts that individuals segregate gains and integrate losses. As a result, Joan, who receives a sudden increase in income during the summer months, is likely to save the surplus and treat it as an asset. On the other hand, John, who earns a steady income, is more likely to follow a standard consumption-smoothing behavior."}]

Q.  Use Caplan's article "Stigler-Becker vs. Myers-Briggs" to argue that, in practice, behavioral anomalies matter much less in the real-world than most behavioral economists suggest.  (Hint: Self-selection)  Give TWO empirically important examples.
Preference Reversals is an anomaly most explained by 5 personal characteristics which determine, as a proxy, your preferences .  The empirical research shows that at 30+ those characteristics become invariant. Therefore we can infer that preferences are steady and Becker and Stigler are defended.  Any empirical estimation of the pay gap that does not include characteristics should be discounted because it determines preferences.  For example your characteristics determine career choice, education have an influence on the pay gap.  Without those in a regression you model cannot be casual.

    "answer": "Preference Reversals, Pay gap estimation",
    "explanation": "Preference Reversals is a behavioral anomaly that can be explained by 5 personal characteristics that act as proxies for preferences. Empirical research shows that these characteristics become invariant at age 30 or above, suggesting that preferences are steady, supporting the Stigler-Becker argument. Q. Standard estimates of the return to education ignore intelligence and assume that all education is productive.
True, False, and Explain: For both of these issues, switching to more realistic assumptions reduces the private and social return to education.
True. Once the return to education controls for IQ, the private return to education falls significantly. Due to labor market asymmetry, employers will pay an educated individual more, but at the expense of other employees. So while there is higher private return, there is a lower, or in the case of including tuition, a negative social return to education.

Q.  Workers deeply resent nominal wage cuts.  Voters deeply resent inflation.  Use these two stylized facts to build a simple, depressing model of the economic and political effects of negative shocks to Aggregate Demand.  Make sure you discuss the interaction between economics and politics.
True. From Kreps: "Ellsberg uses this sort of behavior to claim that there is a real distinction to be made between situations where there is risk (objective uncertainty) and situations with uncertainty (subjective uncertainty). The story goes: people dislike the ambiguity that comes with choice under uncertainty; they dislike the possibility that they may have the odds wrong and so make a wrong choice" (p. 117).

Q. Suppose your U=w.5. You are searching for a job and face the following decision tree:
Step 1: You can either take a sure thing (w=$50,000 with p=1) or keep searching (w=$100,000 with p=.6; w=$0 with p=.4).
Step 2: If you selected the second option, you are allowed to buy an "unemployment insurance" policy. It costs $10,000 and pays $20,000 if you are unemployed.
Step 3: You find out if you got a job. If you did not, you collect the insurance payout. Either way, you have to pay the premium.

Q. Consider an efficiency wage model.
True, False, and Explain:  As the average worker becomes less selfish, the unemployment rate falls. As the average worker becomes more myopic, the unemployment rate rises.
False. The efficiency wage would make him prize his employment more, meaning that if he became more selfish he would strive to remain employed. If he became more myopic, he would work less hard to keep his job, being less cognizant of the fact that there would be some surplus of unemployment due to the efficiency wage and that once he lost his job, he would have a harder time finding another job.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Consider an efficiency wage model. True, False, and Explain: As the average worker becomes less selfish, the unemployment rate falls. As the average worker becomes more myopic, the unemployment rate rises.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "In an efficiency wage model, a less selfish worker would value their employment more and strive to remain employed. On the other hand, a more myopic worker would be less concerned about job security and work less diligently, not fully recognizing the surplus of unemployment due to the efficiency wage. As a result, they may struggle to find another job once unemployed."
  }
