Q.Suppose a woman has EU=w.5 + X. If the woman has a baby boy, X= -10; if she has a baby girl, X=5; if she has no child, X=0. Normally, she is equally likely to have a boy or a girl. But if she pays a price P, she can determine her child's gender with certainty. Suppose further that w initially equals $400, and the P is the only cost associated with having children.
True, False, and Explain: The woman will remain childless if P>87.5.
EU with no child =20
EU with price to guarantee Girl
(400-p)^.5+5
(400-87.5)^.5+5= less than 20
Thus, she won't do it for any price greater that 87.5


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "Suppose a woman has EU=w.5 + X. If the woman has a baby boy, X= -10; if she has a baby girl, X=5; if she has no child, X=0. Normally, she is equally likely to have a boy or a girl. But if she pays a price P, she can determine her child's gender with certainty. Suppose further that w initially equals $400, and the P is the only cost associated with having children. True, False, and Explain: The woman will remain childless if P>87.5.",
    "answer": "True",
    "explanation": "The expected utility with no child is 20, as EU = (400 ^ 0.5) + 0. With a price paid to guarantee a girl, the utility is (400 - p) ^ 0.5 + 5. If P > 87.5, the utility of having a girl is less than 20 ((400 - 87.5) ^ 0.5 + 5 < 20). Therefore, the woman would not pay a price higher than 87.5 and would remain childless."

QT.  True, False, and Explain:  In a general equilibrium framework, "fiscal stimulus" can't work, because the funding for the stimulus package has to come from somewhere.
 False. By changing initial endowments, redistributing in the right, you can make a Pareto efficient allocation self-sustaining.  The "fiscal stimulus" could qualify as such a distribution. This is formally described as the 2nd Welfare Theorem, which says that any Pareto efficient allocation can be a GE, given some initial endowments. Arrow-Debreau extend the 2nd welfare theorem to include inter-temporal exchange, implying that a debt-financed fiscal stimulus could be feasible.   


---

[
  {
    "enuncate": "True, False, and Explain: In a general equilibrium framework, \"fiscal stimulus\" can't work, because the funding for the stimulus package has to come from somewhere.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Fiscal stimulus can work in a general equilibrium framework. By altering initial endowments and redistributing them appropriately, a Pareto efficient allocation can be sustained. The fiscal stimulus could be considered as such a distribution. The Second Welfare Theorem states that any Pareto efficient allocation can be a general equilibrium, provided there are certain initial endowments. Arrow-Debreu extends the Second Welfare Theorem to include intertemporal exchange, suggesting that a debt-financed fiscal stimulus is feasible."
  }
]

Q.  Landsburg ("People Wanted") argues that there are positive externalities of population growth.
True, False, and Explain:  Search theory suggests that Landsburg actually understates the case for higher population.
False.  Search Theory says people search for opportunity up to the point where the expected MB equals expected MC.  Search Theory provides no additional positive externalities for benefits from population growth that are not already captured in Landsburg's benefits.  Landsburgs accounts for greater numbers of geniuses, higher densities, ie less costs to searching.

    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Search Theory states that people search for opportunities until the expected marginal benefit equals the expected marginal cost. It doesn't provide any additional positive externalities for benefits from population growth that are not already captured in Landsburg's benefits. Landsburg's account includes factors such as a greater number of geniuses and higher population densities, which potentially lead to lower search costs."

QET.  Consider a simple asymmetric information model: The "true value" of a company to sellers is uniformly distributed along [0,100], and the value to buyers is 50% more than to sellers.
True, False, and Explain:  In theory, irrationality could increase or decrease market efficiency; in practice, however, experiments show that irrationality makes this "market for lemons" less efficient.
False.  Kreps shows that with asymmetric information, and full rationality, that this market would disappear.  Irrationality therefore, could not decrease something that already disappears. Irrationality could increase market efficiency if it leads buyers to over value goods.   In practice, irrationality is shown to increase efficiency because markets are not bid down to zero in experiments.  
If the value to buyers is 100% more than it is to sellers you end up with ? of the overall market interactions achieved. This is because, the market average is still at 50% and half the distribution is sold.   In other words, value to sellers would be 25, but value to buyers would be 50, since they value it 100% more.  Since buyers would then be willing to spend the average amount, half the distribution, those lower than 50% in the seller's eyes would get sold.

Q.  True, False, and Explain:  Moral hazard models predict that the people with the most insurance will take the most risks, but adverse selection models predict the opposite.
False. Moral Hazzard predicts that "once you insure a risk-adverse agent, they may want to take additional risk." Adverse Selection suggests that people who already possess certain risk factors or already engage in riskier behavior are more likely to buy insurance.  The difference is a matter of post facto behavioral changes versus prior self-selection.


---

[
  {
    "enunciate": "True, False, and Explain: Moral hazard models predict that the people with the most insurance will take the most risks, but adverse selection models predict the opposite.",
    "answer": "False",
    "explanation": "Moral Hazard predicts that once a risk-averse agent is insured, they may want to take additional risks. On the other hand, Adverse Selection suggests that people who already have certain risk factors or engage in riskier behavior are more likely to buy insurance. The distinction lies in the difference between post facto behavioral changes and prior self-selection."
  }
]

Q. True, False, and Explain: Asymmetric information problems do not require irrationality, but irrationality makes asymmetric information problems more severe.

When making choice under uncertainty, we may not know how an individual will act with certainty but with the knowledge of common irrational biases and heuristics, we might better predict his behavior. In other words it depends on the direction of the bias. Lemon Example plays

The severity of asymmetric information problems depends on the direction of the bias."

Q. Taking into account everything you have learned, sketch your best explanation for why the U.S. political system allowed the subprime crisis to happen.
Using a moral hazard argument, we can see that government policy had propped up unsustainable lending and borrowing.  On the lender side it allowed banks to take risks and lend to sub-optimal borrowers.  From the standpoint of buyers, it allowed them to take risks buying houses they couldn't quite afford.  Fannie and Freddie are willing to buy all mortgage loans outright so lenders don't have the risk.  However, once the bill comes due, in the form of default, government passes the risk onto the general public in the form of taxes (Debt, inflation).
This could also be answer in an irrational rationality answer, because the cost of understanding why the bubble is so high.   The American Dream then is a reason for irrational rationality created a basis for that systemic bias.

Q. True, False, and Explain: Prices in betting markets are determined by agents' preferences and endowments, not objective probabilities. However, if some people have more accurate beliefs than others, then over time, prices become more and more likely to reflect objective probabilities.
TRUE.  Your preference for irrationality in betting markets drops because you are internalizing the costs of having irrational beliefs. So to avoid that cost, you start placing bets that are closer to actual probabilities.   The cost of irrational preference indulgence is higher. 


---

To avoid these costs, they begin placing bets closer to actual probabilities. As a result, the cost of indulging in irrational preferences becomes higher, and over time, prices become increasingly likely to reflect objective probabilities."}]

Q. True, False, and Explain: If the efficient markets hypothesis is correct, then the marginal private cost of irrational investing can easily be zero.
 True. It could happen that one's irrational investments could align with the random walk of an efficient market such that the marginal private cost of irrational investing would be zero.  If EMH holds, one could face a marginal private cost of investing of zero.
